#10260. Exploring the Indium phosphide supply chain — the material constraint tightening the optical interconnect bottleneck

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

As the focus goes in on Optical, InP comes under the spotlight.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Indium phosphide (InP) is a compound material used in specialized chip-making. Unlike silicon, which is the fundamental building block for mainstream logic and memory chips, InP — made from indium and phosphorus — is mainly used to manufacture highly specialized photonic and high-frequency electronic chips, largely due to its outstanding optical properties. Its immensely expensive and complex manufacturing process also makes it impractical for the large volumes and die sizes needed for logic and memory chips. With the industry predicting a shortfall of this crucial material right as the shift to optical interconnects in data centers begins, we explore the current state of the supply chain.

InP chips have powered high-speed lasers, modulators, photodetectors, and various other optical components used across the telecommunications industry and defense for decades. While the InP supply chain was relatively small and highly specialized, it was generally stable and sufficient to meet global demands.

Then came the AI boom. As AI clusters hit the physical limits of moving enormous amounts of data over copper, the industry began the switch to optical interconnects. First came ever-faster optical transceivers. Now the industry is transitioning to silicon photonics, near-packaged optics, and co-packaged optics, bringing optical links progressively closer to the processors themselves. InP is central to much of this transition, building the critical active optical components needed to turn electrical data into light and back again.

Individual AI data centers are already being built around hundreds of thousands of accelerators, with upcoming gigawatt-scale buildouts expected to house millions. Connecting those processors translates into demand for millions upon millions of indispensable optical components, all relying on the same relatively tiny InP supply chain.

Japan's Sumitomo Electric and U.S.-headquartered AXT together account for almost 80% of global InP substrate manufacturing, with JX Advanced Metals adding roughly another 10%. AXT, however, manufactures most of its InP substrates in China, where exports now require government licenses, further exacerbating the situation. It gets worse: China accounts for around 70% of global refined indium output, which is recovered as a byproduct of processing zinc. Export controls mean that restrictions apply to both the material itself and the finished wafer.

The resulting squeeze is already sending ripples across the industry, with several companies predicting shortfalls. Michael Hurlston, CEO of optical-component manufacturer Lumentum, said in July that the company's InP fabs were shipping more than 30% below customer demand, despite rapid capacity expansion. Coherent also described InP capacity as the “primary constraint” on its transceiver business.

A simple solution would be to expand the supply chain. However, growing large, low-defect InP crystals is difficult, while qualifying new suppliers requires time that doesn't align with the industry's demand. Furthermore, expanding substrate and laser-fabrication capacity can take years. Meanwhile, to secure optical components capacity for its accelerators, Nvidia has invested $2 billion each in Lumentum and Coherent, attaching multibillion-dollar purchase commitments and future capacity rights to both deals. Both companies, along with several others in the supply chain, are frantically pursuing solutions.

Indium phosphide belongs to the III-V family of compound semiconductors, named after the periodic-table groups of their constituent elements; in InP’s case, indium and phosphorus. Its better-known relatives include gallium arsenide and gallium nitride.

Indium phosphide’s distinguishing property, which separates it from silicon, is its direct bandgap, measured at about 1.34 eV at room temperature. In a direct-bandgap material, electrons dropping across the gap release their energy efficiently as photons, making the material particularly useful for lasers and other active optical devices.

Silicon, on the other hand, has an indirect bandgap, meaning that the same transition requires an additional interaction with a crystal lattice and mostly sheds its energy as heat instead. Therefore, while the material can — in silicon photonics — guide, split, and modulate light using structures fabricated with mature CMOS processes, it does not produce the light beam. As a result, Modern silicon-photonics systems commonly rely on a III-V laser source, most commonly based on the InP material system, to provide the light that the silicon circuitry manipulates.

The fiber-optic wavelength window’s alignment with InP's properties is another reason for the material's ubiquity. Modern telecom and data-center links commonly operate around 1,310 nm and 1,550 nm, where optical fiber exhibits favorable transmission characteristics. Pure InP itself does not emit at those exact wavelengths. However, InGaAsP and InAlGaAs alloys, which are grown on lattice-matched InP substrates, can have their bandgaps engineered for those bands. InGaAs layers on InP are also highly effective photodetectors at telecom wavelengths.

These characteristics and functionalities make the InP platform critical to many active functions in optical communications, including distributed-feedback (DFB) lasers, electro-absorption modulated lasers (EMLs), continuous-wave (CW) lasers, semiconductor optical amplifiers, photodetectors, and complete photonic integrated circuits. Silicon photonics and InP consequently complement each other. Silicon provides scalable optical circuitry, while InP provides efficient light generation and several high-performance active optical devices.

Beyond photonics, InP is also commonly used in high-frequency electronic chips. Electrons move through the material far faster than through silicon, giving it strong high-frequency performance that makes it suitable for millimeter-wave and terahertz electronics, radar receivers, and high-speed data converters. This market is much smaller than photonics, but it has few substitutes at the top of the frequency range.

InP lasers and transceivers have been converting electrical signals into light for long-haul fiber networks, metro links, submarine cables, and eventually shorter-reach data-center connections for decades. Despite 5G rollouts driving demand higher, the indium phosphide supply chain remained a stable, moderately sized business with predictable growth. The AI boom has now driven demand up by several orders of magnitude.

Modern AI clusters can contain hundreds of thousands of interconnected accelerators that continuously exchange data. Copper remains a cheap, effective connector over short distances, but electrical losses, signal integrity, and power requirements progressively worsen as bandwidth and distance increase. This has led to the rapid adoption of optical interconnects.

In this setup, a modulator encodes electrical data from the switch or accelerator onto light from a laser. Fiber cables then carry it across the link to a photodetector that converts the received photons back into an electrical signal. In pluggable transceivers, many of these functions are integrated into a compact module plugged into the front of a network switch, with InP-based components executing several steps in the process.

Lumentum's 200G EML, for example, monolithically combines a wavelength-locked DFB laser and an electro-absorption modulator on its InP platform, and is designed for 200-Gbps-per-lane links used in 1.6T transceivers. Coherent is producing InP EMLs, high-power CW lasers, and high-speed photodiodes for the same generation of hardware. As aggregate link speeds move from 400G and 800G to 1.6T and beyond, the number and performance of these InP-based optical components become increasingly important.

At the same time, the industry is bringing optics right to the board with co-packaged optics (CPO) to further shorten the distance data travels. Conventional pluggable optics leave around 15 to 30 centimeters (5.90 to 11.81 inches) of high-speed copper trace between the switch ASIC and the optical module. CPO brings the optical engine onto the accelerator package as a photonic integrated circuit (PIC) chip, shrinking that electrical path and reducing power consumption. Nvidia's Quantum-X and Spectrum-X photonics switches are among the first major AI platforms built around that approach.

While they integrate several optical functions onto the boards, PIC chips do not generate the laser. CPO and silicon-photonics designs still mostly use external InP CW lasers to supply light to the silicon photonic circuits, further consolidating the demand for indium phosphide in AI data centers and the industry as a whole.

As a result of the enormous, rapidly expanding market for the material, indium phosphide’s supply chain is constrained at several points, starting with the raw material itself. Unlike many metals which are mined from dedicated deposits, indium is typically recovered as a trace byproduct of processing zinc sulfide ore. Manufacturers then combine high-purity indium and phosphorus and grow them into a single-crystal InP ingot, or boule, which they process into substrate wafers with tightly controlled properties. Next, manufacturers grow extremely thin epitaxial layers of InP-related compounds over it, commonly using metal-organic chemical vapor deposition. The epiwafer then goes through several chip-making processes and module assembly to become optical chips and devices.

Indium production's dependence on Zinc processing creates bottlenecks in InP's supply chain, right at the beginning of the stream. The USGS put global primary refined indium production at roughly 1,100 tonnes in 2025, with China accounting for 760 tonnes. For context, the BBC estimates that 30,000 tonnes of electronic-grade silicon used exclusively to make microchips are mined yearly. The United States imports all of the InP it consumes, producing nothing. Meanwhile, indium substrate manufacturers Japan's Sumitomo and JX Metals source more than 90% of their high-purity indium feedstock from China, meaning even non-Chinese substrate makers begin their process with Chinese material.

Further downstream, at the substrate manufacturing level, Sumitomo Electric holds roughly 40% of the global InP substrate market, U.S.-based AXT around 35%, and JX Advanced Metals about 10%. Together, the three control more than 90% of capacity. Although AXT is headquartered in California, most of its InP substrate production runs through its Beijing Tongmei subsidiary, which puts it directly under China's export licensing regime. Other fringe substrate manufacturers include Freiberger Compound Materials in Germany and a growing set of Chinese entrants, including Yunnan Germanium.

The supply chain is also highly concentrated at the device and component level, with Coherent and Lumentum dominating Western laser and component production. IQE supplies epitaxial wafers, and Japanese firms, including Mitsubishi Electric, supply laser components. Chinese module makers such as Innolight and Eoptolink assemble a large share of the world's finished 800G modules, pairing Western lasers with Broadcom and Marvell DSPs. We recently explored how this entanglement complicates any attempt to decouple the two ecosystems, even as the FCC is considering a ban on Chinese optical hardware. Coherent primarily sources substrates from AXT, while Lumentum relies heavily on Sumitomo and JX.

On the technical side, wafer-size limitations in InP semiconductors add another constraint. While silicon logic and memory moved to 300-mm wafers in the early 2000s, the InP industry is only now transitioning from 3-inch to 6-inch (150-mm) production; primarily because the material is brittle, expensive, and grown in small boules, factors that limit wafer size. Doubling the wafer diameter gives four times the area. Coherent's six-inch line, for example, yields more than four times as many devices as its three-inch line at a lower cost per die. On the other hand, moving a fab to six-inch requires new crystal-growth recipes, new handling equipment, new process qualification, and extensive customer requalification.

The supply chain was already struggling under these constraints when, in February 2025, Beijing placed InP, trimethylindium, triethylindium, and the technology used to produce them under export controls, requiring licenses for overseas shipments. AXT called the export controls its most significant current challenge, noting that even with sufficient capacity and strong demand, its ability to ship outside China depends on Chinese licensing decisions.

The widening gap between demand and InP industry output points to a fast-approaching shortfall. Coherent first warned of an InP shortage on its May earnings call. At the close of its fiscal year in August, CEO Jim Anderson described indium phosphide availability as the company's primary constraint, confirming that transceiver assembly and test capacity were available.

Lumentum CEO Hurlston said in July that telecom customers historically ordered lasers in the hundreds, while Nvidia and the hyperscalers were asking for hundreds of millions. Despite operating multiple InP fabs, Lumentum was shipping more than 30% below customer requirements. Earlier in the year, Hurlston said the company's capacity was sold out through 2027 and probably into 2028, even with the expanded output. In both cases, capacity was mostly bought out via Nvidia’s $4 billion investment.

To ease the multifaceted bottleneck, the industry is pursuing several solutions simultaneously, primarily expansions. Coherent is expanding its Sherman, Texas campus, reportedly described as the first and largest high-volume 6-inch InP semiconductor manufacturing facility. Washington signed a letter of intent in June for up to $50 million in CHIPS Act funding for the expansion. Coherent is now on track to double internal InP output in 2026, one quarter ahead of its original plan, and more than double it again by the end of 2027, while also adding 6-inch production in Sweden and Zurich.

Lumentum, meanwhile, is converting a 240,000-square-foot former Qorvo gallium-arsenide facility in North Carolina, which it bought for about $18 million, into another InP fab. The relevance of these expansions largely depends on the downstream InP substrate supply. Lumentum signed an agreement in July to reserve AXT InP substrate capacity through 2031, with an initial $43.5 million deposit and another $43.5 million payment contemplated for 2028. Coherent signed its own three-year 6-inch supply agreement with AXT a month earlier and prepaid $22.3 million against committed capacity.

The substrate suppliers are also expanding in kind. AXT is channeling fresh funds into Beijing Tongmei to double capacity by the end of 2026 and double it again in 2027. Similarly, JX Advanced Metals plans to invest roughly ¥120 billion ($746 million) through FY2030 to lift capacity seven to ten times above FY2025 levels, its largest-ever commitment in semiconductor materials.

Indium production — the uppermost stage of the stream — is far less active, with the biggest hurdle being the reliance on Zinc production. Global zinc mine production hasn't grown materially since 2011, sitting at or below 13 million tonnes a year, according to analysts. Wood Mackenzie expects just 0.1% growth in 2026. On the other hand, not everyone expects the crunch to last. LightCounting estimated in April that optical component demand exceeded supply by about 30% but projected that shortages would largely clear by the end of 2026 as new capacity comes online.

There are also viable, albeit slow, technical workarounds to ease the crunch. Heterogeneous integration bonds InP onto silicon to combine the strengths of both, while quantum dot lasers offer better temperature stability for uncooled operation. Furthermore, researchers continue to push InP quantum dot structures grown directly on silicon substrates. While these do not remove indium from the equation, they change how much each device needs and how it attaches, reducing consumption. Photonics is widely viewed as one of AI's major bottlenecks. However, the indium phosphide supply chain is itself tightening that bottleneck.

#10258. Claude rolling out updated Android app with Material 3 Expressive redesign

Anthropic is rolling out a new Claude app for Android that picks up a Material 3 Expressive redesign.

Confirmed on Twitter/X by Anthropic Product Manager Robert Bye, Claude is now rolling out a Material 3 Expressive redesign for its Android app that, while not a full embrace of Google’s design language, does make a clear attempt to better fit in on Android.

The updated design picks up a bottom-bar UI as opposed to relying fully on the old hamburger menu – it still exists, but it’s mainly for chat history now. You can see M3 elements throughout the app, including in the model switcher and the background of chats. It’s clearly themed to match Anthropic’s aesthetic, and there’s no obvious implementation of Material You colors, but it all fits within the general Material 3 guidelines – much more so than the original app.

The one flaw we spotted was the predictive back implementation, which gets a neat animation to move the search bar but is supposed to show a preview of the previous screen, but that’s just nit-picking, really.

The new Claude app for Android is rolling out now.

#10256. Hands-on: Baseus’ new PicoGo Pro USB-C charger is clever, cute, and on sale

USB-C chargers are pretty standardized at this point, but there’s a fun new trend of adding displays to your power brick that I’ve been very much enjoying, and the new PicoGo Pro from Baseus is a great example with three ports, optimized charging, and a cute little character to top it off – and for Black Friday, it’s pretty deeply discounted, too.

The Baseus PicoGo Pro (AH22) is a 100W, 3-port USB-C charger designed to top off your laptop, phone, and/or tablet, even all at the same time. For anyone who travels often or just has a lot of devices on hand, chargers like this are a must. Having something you don’t have to think twice about when it comes to charging rates is just so nice, and these have come a long way, too.

Using GaN tech, PicoGo Pro is very compact at about 2 inches tall and a little over an inch wide. I remember having single-port chargers that could barely fast-charge my phone that were twice this size just a few years ago. GaN just absolutely rules, and Baseus is using a special version that reduces heat and improves efficiency.

But the two reasons I really like this charger aren’t strictly to do with GaN.

The first is the built-in display. Again, that’s a trend throughout the industry. Anker has several chargers out with built-in displays as the company helped to really make this popular – the Anker Prime 160W brick is really similar to PicoGo Pro (at a higher price), and it’s the only I’ve been using for months before trying out this Baseus model.

Baseus has a cute little animated character that accompanies your charging, with little eyes when the charger isn’t active and some fun animations to show your charging rate in real-time. Neat! You can “press” the button to move through the UI and even see the specific charging rate of each connected device. You’ll also see that the brick can recognize the device you have plugged in, though Android devices just show a generic “SAMSUNG” or whatever brand you plug in – Anker, again, had a similar set of features in a brick released earlier in 2026, but some of those features were iPhone-only, and that brick was only a 1-port, 45W charger anyway.

But, like that Anker brick, Baseus supports “smart” charging that adjusts to your device. Plugging in a Galaxy Z Fold 8 that was around 65% full, I saw the brick adjust its charging speed to tailor to the phone, initially going up to full speed before settling down around 28W~. You can manually adjust this with “Turbo” or “Gentle” modes, the former maxing out the charging rate and the latter settling in around 15W for a slower charge. That flexibility is great because it means you can adjust the charging rate to what you need in that moment. Full-speed for a quick top-off on a busy day, slower charging overnight to preserve battery health.

Baseus isn’t doing much new here, though the GaN tech mentioned earlier is enhanced by some architecture and algorithm adjustments the company has made behind the scenes, but it’s a really good implementation.

Beyond that, it’s also priced pretty well.

Baseus PicoGo Pro’s full price is $59.99, but you’ll probably rarely see it there. Technically, today is its launch date, and it’s already down by over 30% at $39.70 for Black Friday. Value-wise, this one is hard to beat.

#10255. Electronics manufacturers set up memory 'war rooms' to find, lock in DRAM supplies amid worst shortage in history

Even Elon Musk can't seem to get special treatment.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The memory shortage has gotten so bad that many companies, especially smaller ones, have started establishing “procurement war rooms,” where employees are desperately trying to find memory chip supplies on sites like eBay and trying to figure out how they could make manufacturers offer them even just a small amount of DRAM. This massive shortage has pushed up costs for electronic manufacturers, with some companies forced to increase prices to make up the difference while others cut the specifications of their products while introducing a smaller price bump.

The Wall Street Journal reports that Taiwanese memory module and storage maker Apacer had to take out loans just to have money on hand in case it finds a supply of memory chips. “If chips show up, we take it,” Apacer President Gibson Chen told the publication. “This is crazy, but there is no other way.” Even one of the world’s richest people, Elon Musk, was reportedly having trouble finding memory chips. A portfolio manager claimed that Musk offered Samsung an LTA and even a financial guarantee for new Samsung chip factories, but the company’s executive chairman, Lee Jae-yong, allegedly answered that everyone else was offering those things already. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman also recently went to South Korea to meet with Samsung leadership just to secure more RAM for data centers.

Even though PC builders and enthusiasts were the hardest hit by the 500% climb in memory prices, many other electronics manufacturers are also now feeling the squeeze. Big companies like Amazon, Apple, and Microsoft could use their scale and resources to pay more and secure massive amounts of memory chip supplies under long-term agreements (LTAs). However, smaller firms would usually have to rely on brokers and whatever’s available to find what they need. Some have reportedly contacted hundreds of brokers and go through online listings, where they would snap up even small batches of just five or 12 units just to have some supply. Others proceed to the gray market, although they must be careful as some unscrupulous sellers mix in batches of used memory chips with brand new orders to inflate their deliveries.

The scramble for AI compute has caused a massive memory chip shortage, especially as the big three memory chip manufacturers — Micron, Samsung, and SK hynix — have focused their production on more lucrative HBM. Both memory chip manufacturers and RAM kit makers are warning of continued shortages, too, with SK hynix saying 2027 will be the “worst year” for memory shortages, while TeamGroup’s CEO said that it could reach a point where “even money can’t buy goods.” Although the big three manufacturers are building new factories, they’re not expected to go online and start pushing out memory chips until 2028.

It’s difficult for a new player to enter the memory chip market. Aside from the enormous costs involved in setting up a factory and supply chain to build memory chips, any potential entrant must also license a ton of patents and develop its own technologies before it can start making competitive chips. Even if it was able to get its hands on the knowledge required to design memory chips, it still needs the manufacturing expertise needed to produce them at scale. CXMT is the only new player that has successfully produced mainstream consumer DDR5 RAM, but it’s facing multiple allegations that it stole the technology needed to achieve that from Samsung. Even then, the DDR5 memory chips that the Chinese company produced don’t have the same performance as its competitors and track market prices, meaning it’s not the affordable alternative that many tech enthusiasts imagined it to be.

The memory chip market is known for its boom-and-bust cycle, which is why manufacturers were initially reluctant to increase their capacity. But even if they started building factories at the first sign of a shortage, it would still take several years before these new facilities could start pumping out chips to ease the situation. Nevertheless, the three companies are currently facing a price-fixing lawsuit alleging that they coordinated their shift to HBM to cut DDR3 and DDR4 production, with the judge handling the case saying that it’s plausible they colluded as the market experienced the price surge.

#10253. Sizing GPU Memory for a RAG Service: KV Cache, Context and Concurrency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) app is usually drawn as a language model plus a vector database. When it goes live, the part that fails first is rarely the retrieval. It is GPU memory on the generation model, because a model that loads fine for one request can run out of memory when several users send long questions at once.

This article shows how to estimate that limit before you deploy, with a short script you can run, and then covers what else in the pipeline can slow responses down.

Serving a model takes more than its weights. The GPU also holds:

the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with the length of every active request

memory used by the inference runtime itself

SitePoint's guide to running a local LLM on 10GB of VRAM makes the same point for a single desktop GPU: the usable budget is the total VRAM minus overhead, and the KV cache grows with the context window, so the real model budget is smaller than the number on the box.

For a RAG service, the KV cache matters even more. Retrieved passages are pasted into the prompt, so every request carries a long context, and every concurrent request holds its own cache.

The cache size per token depends on the model's architecture: two tensors (keys and values) for every layer, for every KV head. This script turns those numbers into a rough limit on concurrent requests:

def kv_bytes_per_token(layers, kv_heads, head_dim, dtype_bytes=2):

# Keys and values, for every layer, for one token.

return 2 *layers *kv_heads *head_dim *dtype_bytes

def max_concurrent(vram_gb, weights_gb, ctx_tokens, per_token, reserve_gb=2.0):

budget = (vram_gb - weights_gb - reserve_gb) * GIB

return int(budget // (ctx_tokens * per_token))

per_token = kv_bytes_per_token(32, 8, 128)

print(per_token // 1024, "KiB per token")

n = max_concurrent(24, 16, ctx, per_token)

print(ctx, "tokens ->", n, "concurrent requests")

For a 24 GB GPU holding about 16 GB of fp16 weights with 2 GB kept in reserve, it prints:

The numbers are estimates. Real runtimes add their own overhead, and the architecture values come from the model's configuration file, so read them from the model you actually use. The pattern is what matters: doubling the context length halves the number of requests that fit. A RAG prompt that stuffs in ten retrieved chunks costs real capacity.

Serving runtimes do not simply crash when the cache is full. The vLLM documentation on optimization and tuning explains that when KV cache space is insufficient for all batched requests, the engine preempts some of them and recomputes them later, which can hurt end-to-end latency. Its suggested remedies include giving the cache more memory, reducing the number of concurrent sequences or batched tokens, or sharding the model across GPUs.

So the symptom of undersizing is often not an error. It is slow, uneven responses under load. Test with realistic prompt lengths and several simultaneous users, not a single request.

Embedding and ingestion have a different profile from generation. Embedding is often lighter than serving a large language model, and some teams run it on CPU. Document parsing, chunking and tokenization also run on CPU, so a fast GPU cannot make up for a slow CPU upstream. SitePoint's tutorial on a local RAG system for private documents walks through chunking, embeddings, vector storage and reranking, and is a good map of which stages run where.

RAM: vector indexes, document loaders and caches all live in system memory, so large collections need more of it.

Storage: model files, indexes, logs and backups add up. Fast storage shortens model loading and re-indexing.

Network: if the vector database, application server and model server sit in different places, latency adds to every request.

Two layouts are common. A single instance runs everything and is simplest for prototypes and internal tools. Separated services put ingestion and retrieval on smaller machines and keep the GPU for generation, which scales better as traffic grows.

Memory estimates only hold if the GPU memory is really yours. On a shared or time-sliced GPU, other workloads can take memory and compute away from your service, which makes capacity hard to plan. Hosting providers describe this in different terms, such as passthrough, vGPU, MIG and time-slicing. For a view of how one GPU hosting provider explains those allocation methods, GPU Mart's GPU virtualization guide compares them. It is a hosting company's page, so confirm any provider's claims about isolation and performance with your own load test.

Whatever provider you choose, ask these questions:

Is the physical GPU assigned to you exclusively, or partitioned or time-sliced?

Do you have root access to install your own drivers, CUDA libraries and containers?

Which minimum commitments, regions and support terms apply?

A small stack can run as a handful of containers: an API service for request handling and prompt assembly, an ingestion worker, a vector database and a model server. Keeping them separate lets you monitor each one's memory and CPU use and move the heavy one to bigger hardware without rewriting the rest.

For a service that stays up all month, a fixed monthly term is easier to forecast than hourly capacity. Hourly capacity suits testing quantization settings or processing a one-off document batch.

Is GPU memory always the bottleneck for RAG?

Not always. Generation usually hits memory first, but slow parsing, a small CPU or a distant vector database can dominate response time.

Does quantization solve the memory problem?

It shrinks the weights, but the KV cache still grows with context length and concurrency. Test with your real prompt sizes.

It depends on volume. Small workloads can run on CPU, while a large ingestion job may benefit from a GPU for a limited time.

Size a RAG service for the worst realistic moment, not the single-request demo. Estimate the KV cache from the model's architecture, remember that long retrieved contexts reduce how many requests fit, watch for preemption under load, keep CPU, RAM, storage and network in the plan, and find out whether the GPU is exclusive or shared. The script above is small, but it turns a vague worry about memory into numbers you can check.

#10246. From Script to Screen: A Developer's Workflow for Long-Form AI Video

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Generating a single AI video clip is easy now. You send a prompt and get a few seconds of footage back. The trouble starts when the goal is three, five, or ten minutes of video where the same character looks the same in every scene, the style does not drift, and the captions line up with the picture.

Most guides stop at the clip. This one covers the part after it, treating a video as a small build pipeline: a storyboard that is data, prompts generated from that data, clips normalised and stitched with ffmpeg, and captions derived from the same source. If you want to wire a generation model into your own application first, SitePoint's guide to integrating generative video APIs into web applications covers the request and webhook side. If the finished video is headed to a YouTube channel, how to get a YouTube API key and use the Data API is a good starting point for the publishing side. Everything between those two is below.

Long-form AI video fails at the seams between clips, not inside them. Plan for continuity before you generate anything.

A storyboard stored as JSON can be validated, versioned, diffed, and turned into prompts by code, which a pile of loose prompts cannot.

Repeat the full character description in every prompt. Models do not remember the previous clip.

Clips from different generations rarely share resolution, frame rate, or codec settings. Normalise them before joining.

Captions are cheapest when generated from the script and measured clip durations, not transcribed afterwards.

Current video models generate short clips, commonly a handful of seconds each. A longer video is therefore many clips, and every clip is an independent generation. The model has no memory of what the character wore in the previous one.

That produces three kinds of failure. The character drifts, so hair, clothing, or proportions change between scenes. The style drifts, so lighting and colour grade shift from cut to cut. And the technical specs drift, so one clip is 24 frames per second at one resolution and the next is something else. Each of these is fixable, but only if the project is organised so the fixes can be applied consistently.

Start with a structured storyboard instead of a document full of prompts. One file holds the style, a "character bible", and an ordered list of scenes.

"style": "soft watercolor picture book illustration, warm evening light, 16:9",

"mira": "a nine year old girl with short curly black hair, a yellow raincoat and red rubber boots",

"lantern": "a small round paper lantern with a gentle orange glow and a tiny smiling face"

"setting": "a narrow cobblestone street in light rain",

"action": "walks slowly past closed shops, looking up at the sky",

"camera": "slow push in from a medium shot",

"narration": "It had rained all afternoon, and the street was quiet."

"action": "finds the lantern glowing on a wooden crate and kneels beside it",

"narration": "Then she noticed something glowing under the awning."

Because it is data, a script can check it before you spend any generation credits. Two checks pay for themselves quickly: every character named in a scene must exist in the bible, and no scene may be longer than the clip limit of the model you use.

const known = new Set(Object.keys(board.characters));

errors.push${scene.id}: unknown character "${name}");

${scene.id}: ${scene.duration}s is over the ${MAX_CLIP_SECONDS}s clip limit, split it into shots

Treat MAX_CLIP_SECONDS as a setting. Limits differ between models and change over time, so check the one you are using rather than trusting the number in this example.

Never write the per-scene prompt by hand. Generate it, so the style line and the character descriptions are identical every time.

The character description appears word for word in every scene that includes that character. This is the single most effective consistency tactic that needs no special tooling. Changing a detail, say the colour of the boots, becomes one edit in the bible instead of a search through twenty prompts, and the diff shows exactly what changed between versions.

Put the style line first and keep it identical across all scenes.

Describe one action per clip. Two sequential actions in one prompt often produce a blend of both.

Keep camera directions simple. A single move per clip is more reliable than a combination.

Avoid naming real people, brands, or existing characters in prompts. Beyond the rights issue, it makes results harder to reproduce.

Clips from separate generations often disagree on resolution, frame rate, pixel format, or codec parameters. Joining them directly can produce stutters, green frames, or an outright error. Convert every clip to one agreed format first.

-vf "scale=1280:720:force_original_aspect_ratio=decrease,pad=1280:720:(ow-iw)/2:(oh-ih)/2,fps=24,format=yuv420p" \

-an -c:v libx264 -crf 18 "norm/$(basename "$f")"

The filter scales each clip to fit a 1280 by 720 frame, adds padding if the aspect ratio differs, sets 24 frames per second, and forces a widely compatible pixel format. The -an flag drops any audio the model generated, since narration will be added as one track later. Name clips with zero padded numbers such as s01.mp4 and s02.mp4, so a shell glob sorts them in story order.

Once every file shares the same parameters, the concat demuxer can join them without re-encoding:

for f in norm/*.mp4; do echo "file '$PWD/$f'"; done > list.txt

ffmpeg -y -f concat -safe 0 -i list.txt -c copy stitched.mp4

Hard cuts are the safe default. Crossfades are possible with ffmpeg's xfade filter, but they require careful offset arithmetic and a re-encode, so add them only when a cut genuinely looks wrong.

Captions are easiest when they come from the script, not from a speech recognition pass over the finished video. The storyboard already holds the narration text and each scene's length, so an SRT file is a few lines of code. Use measured durations rather than planned ones, because a generated clip is often a little shorter or longer than requested.

const { execFileSync } = require('node:child_process');

const ms = Math.round(totalSeconds * 1000);

const h = String(Math.floor(ms / 3600000)).padStart(2, '0');

const m = String(Math.floor((ms % 3600000) / 60000)).padStart(2, '0');

const s = String(Math.floor((ms % 60000) / 1000)).padStart(2, '0');

const rest = String(ms % 1000).padStart(3, '0');

const end = cursor + scene.duration; // set from measureSeconds() after generation

${blocks.length + 1}\n${toTimestamp(start)} --> ${toTimestamp(end)}\n${scene.narration}\n

Update each scene's duration from measureSeconds on the normalised clip before calling buildSrt. If a narration line is long, split it across two caption blocks so it stays readable. Then combine picture, voiceover, and captions in one final step:

ffmpeg -y -i stitched.mp4 -i narration.mp3 -i captions.srt \

-c:v copy -c:a aac -c:s mov_text -shortest final.mp4

Soft captions like these can be switched off by the viewer and read by assistive technology, which is better than burning text into the picture. Keep the .srt file as well, since most platforms accept it as a separate upload.

Everything above is a good fit when you need repeatability: many videos from templates, versioned storyboards, automated publishing, or tight control over the model. The price is that you own the glue code, the ffmpeg settings, and the failures.

For one off projects, or when the people making the video are not developers, a managed platform is often the more sensible choice. Tools such as magiclight.ai bundle story to video generation, character handling, voice, and an editor in one interface, and advertise videos of up to 50 minutes. Check the specifics yourself, because claims like that are marketing, and results depend on your content. One point matters for developers in particular: if you need to automate generation from your own code, confirm that the platform you pick offers an API or export route before you build a workflow around it. A tool with a good editor and no programmatic access suits a different job than the pipeline in this article.

A short manual pass catches what automation does not. Watch the whole video once at normal speed, looking only at continuity. Read the captions against the audio. Check that no real person's likeness, brand mark, or protected character slipped into the footage. Finally, check the disclosure rules of the platform you publish on, since many now ask creators to label realistic synthetic media, and the rules change.

The model is only one component of a long-form AI video. The reliability comes from the structure around it: a validated storyboard, prompts generated from it, clips forced into one format, and captions derived from measured data. Each of those is ordinary software engineering, and none of it depends on which generation model you choose, so the pipeline survives when the model changes.

Start with a two minute video and four or five scenes. Fix the seams there before scaling up, because every continuity problem you find at that size is one you will not have to hunt for in a ten minute project.

#10245. Nvidia proves it’s too big to fail

Good morning. Today I’m deciding whether the final Avengers: Doomsday trailer passes the “people just standing around” test, trying the new Material 3 Expressive Claude app on my Android devices, and stocking up on discounted XTEINK e-readers for Christmas gifts.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Eleven days after leaving Nvidia, Jensen Huang is back as president, CEO, and a board director. The board voted unanimously to reinstate him on Monday, saying it was satisfied with his candor and ability to lead following a series of meetings. It also said input from senior levels of the US government left it confident that his reinstatement poses no national security risk.

I didn’t see this coming. Huang was dishonest to the public and to his board. He admitted to having affairs not only with an alleged Chinese spy, but also with an employee who directly reported to him. He is accused of facilitating the sale of Nvidia AI chips to companies like Megaspeed that knowingly purchased them with the intention of smuggling them into China, a contravention of US trade restrictions.

As recently as November 17th, the board chair, Harvey Jones, reiterated that the company had no intention of bringing back Huang as CEO. But things changed quickly on Sunday as President Trump reportedly reassured the board that Huang’s reinstatement posed no security risk to US intelligence, something that the new Democratic-led Congress will be sure to test via repeated subpoenas in the new year.

As Huang walked into the atrium at Nvidia’s headquarters, staff gathered around to cheer his return, and he addressed the crowd by saying he would be a “better CEO the second time around,” as if 11 days of unemployment had given him sufficient time to evaluate his past behavior.

Nvidia’s stock was up over 30% on Monday, rebounding from a disastrous end to last week that wiped out $4 trillion in Nasdaq value. It’s hard not to see Huang’s return as a direct response to this short-term disruption, which also hinted at a much more serious correction in the stock market. Nvidia’s stock had already fallen 24% between the day Huang resigned and Friday’s crunch, and with looming regulation from Congress in 2027, a rudderless AI industry could have been disastrous for the US economy writ large.

Though things may appear to be back to normal, or as normal as possible given the insanity of the past couple of weeks, Huang has returned to a very different trade environment. While he was away, Nvidia’s board formed a National Security and Export Control Committee to investigate whether the company’s actions violated any US embargoes. And as part of Huang’s return, Nvidia has committed to hiring its first chief compliance officer, an executive who will ostensibly not report directly to the CEO.

I was pretty gobsmacked when I saw the news of Huang’s return come across the wire at 9am ET yesterday. I’m still wrapping my head around it. Is Nvidia too big to fail, or just a symptom of a larger interdependency between the US executive branch and Big Tech? Huang promises to be a better leader this time around, but to whom: his employees, his customers, or his shareholders? Because he appears to be a very different person to each of those groups.

Better-tasting water for your morning coffee, everyday cooking, or a house full of holiday guests? Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday sale has options for all three, from high-capacity under-sink reverse osmosis systems to installation-free countertop models. Upgrade your kitchen with the X12 under-sink system for $899, save space with the compact G6A6 for $189.99, or skip the plumbing with the M6HG countertop system for $299, which delivers filtered hot water for coffee and tea in seconds.

Whichever setup fits your routine, use code 9TO5GBF26 to take an extra 5% off those sale prices. Waterdrop’s offers also extend to whole-home filtration and replacement refrigerator filters, giving you plenty of ways to upgrade without stretching your holiday budget. Shop Waterdrop’s Black Friday deals on Amazon before the sale ends November 30th.

A discounted Pixel: The Pixel 10 at $600 is a really good deal. Recent software updates have closed some of the feature gaps with the Pixel 11, and the hardware is still solid even a year after launch. If you want better cameras, the Pixel 11 Pro at $749 is an absolute steal.

The gaming-tablet deal: The Legion Tab Gen 5 at $599 makes much more sense than it did at $899: an 8.8-inch 165Hz display, a 12GB/256GB configuration, and an included folio. It’s a good fit for someone specifically seeking a compact Android gaming machine.

The last-generation watch: The 40mm Galaxy Watch 8 at $199 is the sort of older smartwatch worth considering when annual upgrades are modest. And because the Watch 9 is basically identical, I’d go for the Watch 8. If you are looking at Samsung’s latest and greatest, the Galaxy Watch 9 is $379 with a free $50 Amazon gift card, which is a decent deal, too.

A deal on the year’s best Android foldable: The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is showing up at $1,499.99 for some lucky shoppers and $1,549.99 for the rest. Either way, it’s Samsung’s best product of 2026, and my current favorite gadget.

Google’s latest mid-sized sedan AI model is taking the world by storm. Gemini 4 Neon is technically the middle sibling in Google’s lineup, with Carbon at the top and Hydrogen at the bottom, but everyone seems to agree it absolutely cooks.

If you’ve spent any time on X over the past few days (I hate myself for writing that), you’ll likely have seen an uptick in fervor over Google’s latest model, which seems to outperform Claude Opus 5.5, and even Gemini 4 Carbon itself, in many benchmarks and real-world tests. Even Google is providing some helpful guidance about when to use one or the other.

One thing I’ve read again and again about Neon is how adept it is at writing. I’m of two minds about this: the better an AI gets at doing my job, the less necessary I am at said job. On the other hand, I’m so tired of obvious AI slop that a model that actually writes well sounds like a relief. At least when I’m unemployed, I won’t be yelling at the screen all day about why the robot yet again used negative parallelism.

I didn’t get to play much GTA VI over the weekend, but I know a few (million) people who did. Publisher Take-Two says the game generated $3.79 billion in first-day retail sales. That’s an incredible number in any industry, but a truly mind-blowing one in the video game world. Rockstar also released its first patch for the game on Monday to fix a modest number of bugs with the launch code.

If you follow open-weight AI models, you’ll likely have heard of how influential Z.ai’s GLM-5.2 was when it launched in June. Now the company has released GLM-5.4, and it’s novel as much for its architecture as it is for its benchmarks.

Its Loop Transformer reuses a block of layers to do more computation without adding parameters. The company is pitching applications beyond software, including games, visual work, and CAD. The 750-million-parameter model reportedly performs at the level of a 2-3 trillion parameter non-looping equivalent, but we’re still waiting on a broader release for real-world testing.

#10244. TPU Model Design Considerations and Optimization Guide

Google designed TPUs from the ground up to be exceptionally efficient for machine learning workloads, delivering top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Because TPUs trade general-purpose hardware scheduling for wide systolic arrays, dedicated SparseCore engines, and custom interconnect fabrics, adhering to specific architectural best practices is essential to achieve maximum performance. To help developers get the most out of TPUs, we are launching the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign guide. This post highlights its key principles—spanning shape alignment, architectural choices like LatentMoE, and parallelism strategies—to accelerate LLMs, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, and other hardware-intensive workloads on TPUs.

Performance tuning starts with model definition. Architectural choices made before the first training step set permanent ceilings on MFU (Model FLOP Utilization), cost, and serving latency. This guide gives concrete, hardware-grounded design rules for teams building or adapting models for TPUs, organized into three groups:

How to use this guide. The shape rules apply to anyone designing a new model. If you are adapting an existing model rather than designing one, the Expressing Parallelism section applies without touching the checkpoint. The general rules hold for any large-matmul architecture (dense LLMs, multimodal, and world models), and the companion AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository grows model- and workload-specific sections over time.

Eighth-generation TPUs. These principles carry forward to TPU 8t and TPU 8i and to the newly announced TPU 8tx and TPU 8i Max. The reasoning stays the same and the thresholds move with the hardware. TPU 8t and TPU 8i both add native FP4. For training, TPU 8t also brings more balanced Vector Processing Unit (VPU) scaling. For serving, TPU 8i adds the Boardfly topology, a Collectives Acceleration Engine (CAE), and 3x more on-chip SRAM. We note the effects inline in Principles 5–8, and follow-up posts will cover eighth-generation designs in depth.

Measured evidence. Alongside the published sources, notes marked Measured carry results from our own Ironwood profiling program: open 20–30B MoEs (a Qwen3-30B-derived LatentMoE variant; GPT-OSS-20B) plus dense controls, single slice (except for the 512-chip TPU v7x and 1,024-chip TPU v4 ablations in Principle 4), BF16, training shapes up to 32K sequence, all against tuned-systems baselines. Treat the trends and rankings as transferable, the exact percentages as config-specific.

TPUs are purpose-built custom ML ASICs designed from the ground up to deliver state-of-the-art performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). While their architectural choices—such as wide 256×256 systolic arrays, software-managed VMEM, dedicated SparseCore engines, and direct multi-dimensional ICI fabrics—enable exceptional efficiency, they inherently create specific structural constraints and programming patterns that must be carefully followed to unlock maximal performance. Reaching peak efficiency requires aligning model shapes, routing, and parallelism directly with these underlying hardware characteristics:

Make d_model, FFN and expert intermediate widths (d_ff), latent widths, attention projections, and padded vocabulary sizes (used as contracting or output dimensions) multiples of 256 to match the 256×256 MXU systolic array. Token/batch axes and counts such as heads, layers, and experts are exempt. The most frequently missed dimension is head_dim: at 64 or 128, flash-attention QK products leave the MXU ≥ 50% idle. Reshape to head_dim = 256 with proportionally fewer Q and KV heads, preserving total projection widths and logical KV cache bytes (iso-quality in our ablations).

Measured: Reshaping Qwen3-30B MoE attention from 32 × 128 to 16 × 256 (identical parameters and FLOPs) sped up end-to-end training, with gains widening at longer context:

These rules apply for non-quadratic attention as well, notably GDN ablations from Yang et al. 2025 also noted at par or better quality for head dimension 256 for their Gated DeltaNet Attention.

XLA compiles each program for exact tensor shapes, so every buffer has a fixed address and every loop a known trip count, and a new shape means a recompilation and a host stall. What about dynamic shapes? Dynamic workloads fit this model: the trick is to separate the shape of a buffer from the extent of the data inside it.

Instead of padding to the largest group or the longest sequence, we keep the buffers at fixed shapes (tokens sorted by expert and padded only at the tail, the KV cache as a fixed pool of pages) and describe the ragged structure with a few small integer arrays (group sizes and offsets; KV lengths, the page table, cumulative query lengths). Pallas copies these into the TensorCore's scalar memory before the grid starts (PrefetchScalarGridSpec), and the kernel reads them to choose the next tile and bound its loops, with a mask covering a tile that straddles a group boundary or a partly filled last page. The data dependence becomes an address and a trip count instead of a shape, so the compiled program is the same for every routing and every batch composition and the kernel runs at the unpadded FLOP count.

Standard EP dispatches every routed token at full d_model width, making EP + FSDP slower than plain FSDP in our Ironwood benchmarks. LatentMoE projects routed tokens from d_model down to latent width ℓ (d/ℓ ≈ 2–4×) before dispatch, executes experts in latent space, and projects back up after combine (introduced by Nemotron 3 and adopted by 962B MAI-Base-1 and 2.8T Kimi K3):

Measured: On a 30B / 128-expert MoE, an ℓ = 768 (2.67×) LatentMoE cut on-device permute time 2.8×, lifted end-to-end training throughput +14% tokens/s (322 vs 270 TFLOP/s/chip, or +19%, since the latent projections add ~5% FLOPs per token; tokens stay ℓ-wide past the down-projection, so on-device permute and combine shrink alongside EP all-to-all), and freed ~25 GB/device of activation HBM when remat checkpoints were anchored on the compressed latent. While current MoE recipes in tpu-recipes run FSDP (+ TP), LatentMoE + FP8 dispatch makes EP roofline-viable when per-device batch is too small to hide FSDP weight all-gathers. Under both FSDP and EP, avoid over-fragmenting experts: small tokens × K / N group sizes starve MXU tiles.

When candidate MoE layouts match on quality, pick the one with higher compute intensity at a similar active-parameter budget. A larger shared expert (wider d_shared) shifts work into dense, communication-free GEMMs that overlap EP dispatch/combine in training and amortize weight reads across the decode batch. Fewer, wider routed experts (halving N and K while doubling d_ff at fixed sparsity K/N) cut dispatch traffic in half and double per-expert GEMM fill. Optimal (N, K) grows with model size, and over-coarsening hurts routing diversity. Ablate expert granularity and the shared-to-routed split at your target scale rather than copying GPU co-designed models, since the TPU optimum is more shared-heavy.

Measured: On a 63B LatentMoE (ℓ = 768, K/N = 1/32, 8K context, 512 TPU v7x chips at EP = 128), halving (N, K) from (512, 16) to (256, 8) while doubling d_ff and the shared expert lifted training throughput +57% tok/s and held lower loss across 120.8B tokens:

Low-concurrency decode streams model weights every token (~300 concurrent tokens per weight shard are needed in dense FP8 to reach compute roofline). Combine two architectural levers with explicit concurrency sizing:

Norms, activations, masking, routing math and quantization scale applies run on the Vector Processing Unit (VPU), which is roughly two orders of magnitude below the MXU in throughput and executes in sequence with the matmuls unless XLA fuses it into an adjacent GEMM or we move it off the TensorCore. Therefore we drop reshapes and transposes that carry no quality, standardize on fusable RMSNorm and SwiGLU so the elementwise work folds into the neighbouring GEMM, and offload the MoE sort, gather and scatter to the SparseCore.

Note that fused VPU work is free only while its cycle count fits inside the MXU window it is fused into. A quick illustration of this concept is block-scaled fp8 GEMM.

With a scale per block of B elements along the contraction axis, the MXU emits one partial sum per output element per block and the VPU rescales each one (about three vector ops), so per output element the MXU does 2K flops and the VPU does 3·ceil(K/B) ops. The rescale hides when B ≥ 1.5 × r_mxu / r_vpu, which on v7x (fp8 peak 4,614 TFLOPS against a 19.2 TFLOPS vector peak, a ratio near 240) puts the threshold near 340. In simulation on a DeepSeek-V3 expert shape, block 512 runs at the per-tensor time, block 256 is VPU-bound at 1.2x and block 128 at 2.4x. Achieved MXU efficiency is lower than peak, which lowers the effective ratio and can make the smaller blocks workable; the way to decide is to measure the GEMM at each block instead of using peak ratio. This threshold is specific to Ironwood. TPU 8t is designed with specialized support for scaling so quantization for MXFP4 and MXFP8 formats has no overhead. Re-measure the block-size threshold there instead of reusing 340. The rule itself carries forward: keep vector work hidden inside a matmul window.

For workloads dominated by BF16-sensitive matmuls (most notably long-context quadratic attention where direct FP8 degrades accuracy), native single-pass BF16 leaves MXU capacity unused whenever hardware low-precision MXU throughput exceeds native BF16 throughput by more than the pass count. Use the multi-pass BF16 algorithm to execute BF16-accurate matmuls via multiple passes over the higher-throughput lower-precision MXU pipeline without losing numerical fidelity. This matters more as low-precision throughput pulls ahead of BF16. TPU 8t adds native FP4, which doubles MXU throughput. On Ironwood the FP8 to BF16 ratio is exactly 2:1, so two or more passes cannot beat native BF16.

Assign communication-heavy parallel axes to the fastest physical links and keep logical mesh factors aligned with torus dimensions:

Minimum-degree CP matched to kernel trade-offs: All-gather CP is simplest (though its all-gathers can sit exposed) when paired with zigzag load balancing (context_parallel_load_balance in MaxText) so causal masking does not idle tail ranks; Ring CP overlaps collectives but trades off SplashAttention chunk efficiency; Ulysses A2A CP avoids causal skew but exposes all-to-alls and caps at head count.

Start every project with the quality-safe foundation: 256-aligned dimensions (1), static shapes with dropless routing (2), one parallelism plan mapped to physical links (8), wall-clock ablations on a tuned stack, and published reference targets (both in Measuring what matters below). For MoEs, add LatentMoE (3) after an ablation to cut routing/dispatch traffic and decode weight reads. Then prioritize by bottleneck regime:

Google engineered TPUs from the ground up as custom ML ASICs to deliver top-tier performance per total cost of ownership (TCO). Unlocking that potential requires bridging the gap between hardware architecture and model design. By standardizing on 256-aligned shapes, leveraging LatentMoE, and mapping parallel axes directly to physical links, engineering teams can maximize TPU efficiency across both training and serving workloads.

A reference blueprint is only as good as the loop used to validate it. Always evaluate architectural changes in wall-clock time on a tuned software stack rather than paper FLOPs: in our Ironwood benchmarks, pure systems tuning (SparseCore offload, MXU-aligned tiles, and remat tuning) lifted an open 30B MoE from 12% to 23% MFU before any model change, and published Ironwood baselines show 256-aligned MoEs (DeepSeek-V3, Qwen3-235B at 608–630 BF16 TFLOP/s/chip) running at roughly 2× the throughput of unaligned GPT-OSS-120B (330 TFLOP/s/chip). For the 6-signal XProf (profiler=xplane) triage checklist, roofline and HBM budgeting rules, wall-clock ablation methodology, published Ironwood reference targets, and ongoing updates as new TPU generations and kernels land, explore the living diagnostics guide and companion patterns in the open-source AI-Hypercomputer/tpu-codesign repository.

The market is getting nervous with all the shenanigans involving Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

S&P Global Ratings, one of the world’s largest rating agencies, announced that it’s putting Nvidia’s credit rating on watch as the company faces a possible downgrade. The agency says that the issues surrounding Jensen Huang regarding export control violations, as well as his resignation and subsequent reinstatement 11 days later, reveal the weakness of Nvidia’s management team and board oversight.

“We believe the long tenure of certain board members may have compromised their independence,” the rating agency said in its press release. It also added, “Issues related to Nvidia’s China exposure also raise questions regarding geopolitical risk management and internal controls. While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the ‘key man’ risk to the forefront. The departure, then reinstatement of the CEO, does not sufficiently answer our questions regarding board oversight of executive code of conduct.”

Nvidia CEO and founder Jensen Huang resigned from the job on November 12, after multiple allegations of affairs, especially with Megaspeed CEO Alice Huang, who’s also accused of enabling the smuggling of $7.5 billion worth of AI GPUs into China. Even though Huang left the company he founded in disgrace, he was reinstated to the position by the Nvidia board on November 23. This was a major surprise, especially as it had previously refused to bring him back due to the ongoing national security investigation into Jensen.

However, it seems that Nvidia’s board of directors has folded to the pressure, especially after President Donald Trump himself said that he talked to them directly, with the board saying that it “received input from senior levels of the United States” that “Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.” Despite those assurances, the Nvidia chief still faces multiple investigations, including an internal inquiry at Nvidia, a private meeting with the House of Representatives’ Select Committee on China, and a public testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee, among others.

Even though the AI chipmaker’s risk profile remains unchanged, S&P Global Ratings’ warning is a sign that the market is uneasy about the situation regarding stricter export controls and the allegations of chip diversion. The ratings agency said that the downgrade could happen in the next 90 days after it concludes its due diligence, although it expects any potential drop to “be limited to one notch, unless investigations or disclosures lead us to believe Nvidia’s reputation or future business prospects have been harmed.”
