#10199. What Is BIO Tagging and How to Use It for Document AI Training Data

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

If you've worked with named entity recognition (NER) in text, you've probably seen BIO tagging before. It's the standard way to mark where entities start, continue, and end inside a sequence of tokens. What you might not know is that the same tagging scheme has become the backbone of document AI -- the field where machine learning models extract structured data from invoices, receipts, contracts, and other business documents.

This guide explains how BIO tagging works, why it matters for document understanding, and how to build BIO-tagged training data that actually produces accurate models. We'll walk through the tagging logic, show real examples on financial documents, cover common mistakes that silently hurt model performance, and look at tools that speed up the annotation process.

BIO stands for Beginning, Inside, Outside. It's a labeling scheme that tells a model which tokens belong to an entity, which tokens continue that entity, and which tokens are just background noise.

● B (Beginning) -- the first token of a named entity

● I (Inside) -- any token that continues the same entity

● O (Outside) -- any token that doesn't belong to any entity

Take a simple line from an invoice: "Invoice #INV-2024-0891". If you're labeling invoice numbers, the BIO tags look like this:

Token          Label ─────          ───── Invoice        O #              O INV-2024-0891  B-invoice_number

Now consider a multi-word entity like a vendor name: "Acme Corporation Ltd". All three words are part of the same entity, so the tagging becomes:

Token          Label ─────          ───── Acme           B-vendor_name Corporation    I-vendor_name Ltd            I-vendor_name

The B prefix marks where the entity starts. The I prefix marks every following token that belongs to the same entity. Everything else gets O. This three-label structure is simple, but it gives models enough information to learn entity boundaries precisely -- even when two entities of the same type appear next to each other.

Traditional NER works on plain text -- a flat sequence of words. Document AI is harder because pages have two dimensions. A word's meaning depends not just on what comes before and after it in reading order, but also on where it sits on the page. The number "$4,250.00" could be an invoice total, a line item price, or a tax amount depending on its position relative to headers and columns.

Models like LayoutLMv3, LiLT, and BROS solve this by combining text tokens with their bounding box coordinates. Each token gets three inputs: the word itself, its position on the page (x, y, width, height), and its BIO label during training. The model learns to associate spatial patterns with entity types.

BIO tagging fits this architecture perfectly because it operates at the token level. Every word on the page gets exactly one label, and the model learns from thousands of these labeled tokens which spatial and textual patterns correspond to which fields.

If you're coming from a background in natural language processing in Python, the concept is identical -- BIO tagging works the same way whether your tokens come from a sentence or a document page. The difference is that document AI adds positional data to each token.

BIO isn't the only tagging scheme. You'll encounter several variants:

● BIO (IOB2) -- B marks the first token, I marks continuation tokens, O marks non-entities. The most widely used scheme.

● BIOES -- adds E (End) for the last token of a multi-word entity and S (Single) for single-token entities. More explicit boundary marking.

● BILOU -- adds L (Last) and U (Unit). Functionally identical to BIOES with different letters.

● IOB1 -- the original scheme where B is only used when two entities of the same type are adjacent. Rarely used today.

For document AI, standard BIO (IOB2) is the safest choice. Here's why:

● LayoutLMv3 and most pre-trained document models expect BIO format out of the box

● The FUNSD and CORD benchmark datasets use BIO, so your data format matches the academic baselines

● BIOES adds more label types, which means you need more training data to learn each one reliably

● Converting from BIO to BIOES is trivial if you later need it, but the reverse can lose information

Unless your model architecture specifically requires BIOES or BILOU, stick with BIO.

Before you tag a single token, you need a clear label taxonomy -- the list of entity types your model will learn to recognize. A poorly designed taxonomy creates confusion during annotation and cascades into model errors.

Ask yourself: what fields does the downstream system need? For invoice processing, a typical taxonomy looks like this:

vendor_name vendor_address invoice_number invoice_date due_date line_item_description line_item_quantity line_item_price subtotal tax total payment_method currency

**Rule 1 -- One label per semantic meaning. **Don't reuse amount for both line item prices and the invoice total. A model that learns "amount" means two different things depending on context will confuse them on novel layouts.

**Rule 2 -- Use hierarchical names. **Once your taxonomy exceeds 10 labels, flat names start colliding. line_item_price is better than price because it's self-documenting and prevents collisions with total_price.

**Rule 3 -- Include a catch-all label. **Call it other or O. Text that doesn't belong to any entity still needs a label for token classification. Without negative examples, the model can't learn what isn't a field.

**Rule 4 -- Match your model architecture. **Token classification models like LayoutLMv3 work best with fine-grained, word-level labels. Generative models like Donut output structured text, so they work with coarser labels. Choose your model first, then design labels to match.

Here's the practical workflow for turning raw PDF documents into BIO-tagged training data ready for model training.

Your model needs both the text and its coordinates on the page. For digitally generated PDFs, extract the text layer directly:

import pdfplumber def extract_words(pdf_path):     words = []     with pdfplumber.open(pdf_path) as pdf:         for page in pdf.pages:             for word in page.extract_words():                 words.append({                     'text': word['text'],                     'bbox': [word['x0'], word['top'],                              word['x1'], word['bottom']],                     'page': page.page_number                 })     return words

For scanned documents, you'll need OCR. Tesseract is the standard open-source option:

import pytesseract from pdf2image import convert_from_path def ocr_pdf(pdf_path, dpi=300):     images = convert_from_path(pdf_path, dpi=dpi)     all_words = []     for i, img in enumerate(images):         data = pytesseract.image_to_data(             img, output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT         )         for j in range(len(data['text'])):             if data['text'][j].strip():                 all_words.append({                     'text': data['text'][j],                     'bbox': [data['left'][j], data['top'][j],                              data['left'][j] + data['width'][j],                              data['top'][j] + data['height'][j]],                     'page': i + 1                 })     return all_words

Models expect coordinates normalized to a 0--1000 range. This makes the model resolution-independent -- a word at the top-left corner is at (0, 0) whether the page is 72 DPI or 300 DPI.

def normalize_bbox(bbox, page_width, page_height):     return [         int(1000 bbox[0] / page_width), *        int(1000 *bbox[1] / page_height),         int(1000 bbox[2] / page_width), *        int(1000 *bbox[3] / page_height)     ]

If you've worked with bounding box annotation for document AI, you'll recognize this normalization step -- it's the same coordinate transformation used across all document understanding models.

This is the annotation step. For each word in the document, assign the appropriate BIO label based on what field it represents. Here's what a fully labeled invoice line looks like:

words = [     {'text': 'Invoice',      'label': 'O'},     {'text': 'Date:',        'label': 'O'},     {'text': 'March',        'label': 'B-invoice_date'},     {'text': '15,',          'label': 'I-invoice_date'},     {'text': '2024',         'label': 'I-invoice_date'},     {'text': 'Total:',       'label': 'O'},     {'text': '$4,250.00',    'label': 'B-total'},     {'text': 'Acme',         'label': 'B-vendor_name'},     {'text': 'Corporation',  'label': 'I-vendor_name'}, ]

Notice that "March 15, 2024" is a single entity spanning three tokens. The first token gets B-invoice_date, and the following tokens get I-invoice_date. The model learns that these tokens form one continuous entity.

LayoutLMv3 expects training data in a specific JSON structure. Each document becomes one training example with parallel arrays for words, bounding boxes, and labels:

{   "id": "invoice_001",   "words": ["Invoice", "Date:", "March", "15,", "2024",              "Total:", "$4,250.00", "Acme", "Corporation"],   "bboxes": [[84, 42, 145, 58], [84, 62, 120, 78],              [130, 62, 172, 78], [175, 62, 195, 78],              [198, 62, 230, 78], [84, 320, 125, 336],              [130, 320, 210, 336], [84, 22, 125, 38],              [130, 22, 210, 38]],   "labels": ["O", "O", "B-invoice_date", "I-invoice_date",              "I-invoice_date", "O", "B-total",              "B-vendor_name", "I-vendor_name"] }

If you're working with large volumes of documents, this guide on building JSON training datasets from PDFs covers how to automate much of this conversion pipeline.

Manually labeling every word on every page is slow. A single invoice might have 200-400 tokens, and you need hundreds of labeled documents for a usable model. Here are three approaches to speed things up.

Many financial fields follow predictable patterns. Dates, currency amounts, and invoice numbers can be pre-labeled with regex before a human annotator reviews them:

import re PATTERNS = {     'invoice_date': [         r'\b\d{1,2}[/-]\d{1,2}[/-]\d{2,4}\b',         r'\b(?:Jan|Feb|Mar|Apr|May|Jun|Jul|Aug|Sep|Oct|Nov|Dec)\w*\s+\d{1,2},?\s+\d{4}\b'     ],     'total': [         r'$[\d,]+.\d{2}\b'     ],     'invoice_number': [         r'\b(?:INV|inv)[- ]?\d{4,}\b'     ] } def pre_label(words):     for word in words:         word['label'] = 'O'  # default         for label, patterns in PATTERNS.items():             for pattern in patterns:                 if re.match(pattern, word['text']):                     word['label'] = f'B-{label}'                     break     return words

This won't catch everything -- vendor names, addresses, and line item descriptions need human judgment. But it can pre-fill 30-40% of the labels on a typical invoice, turning the annotator's job from labeling to reviewing.

Train a preliminary model on your first 50 labeled documents, then use it to predict labels on new documents. The annotator reviews and corrects the predictions instead of labeling from scratch. As the model improves, corrections become rarer and annotation speed increases.

The key is selecting documents where the model is least confident for the next round of human review. This focuses annotator time where it has the most impact on model accuracy.

If you're working with standard financial documents and want to skip the manual pipeline entirely, platforms like AI Asset Management can auto-generate structured labels from raw PDFs -- typically returning results in under 30 seconds per document. The annotator's role shifts from labeling to quality review, which is significantly faster than tagging every token by hand.

These are the errors that show up most often when teams start building document AI training data. Each one silently degrades model performance.

If "Acme Corporation" is labeled as one entity on page 1 but "Acme" and "Corporation" are labeled as separate entities on page 5, the model receives contradictory training signals. Pick a convention and enforce it across your entire dataset. For vendor names, include the full legal name ("Acme Corporation Ltd") every time.

A common beginner mistake is labeling every token of a multi-word entity with B instead of using B for the first token and I for the rest. "March 15, 2024" should be B-date, I-date, I-date -- not B-date, B-date, B-date. The B-B-B pattern tells the model these are three separate entities, which is wrong.

The word "Total:" is a header, not a value. Only the actual value ($4,250.00) should be tagged as B-total. Labeling "Total:" as part of the entity teaches the model to include headers in its extractions, which corrupts downstream processing.

Some OCR engines split punctuation into separate tokens. "$4,250.00" might become ["$", "4,250", ".", "00"]. If you label only "4,250" and skip the dollar sign and cents, the model learns incomplete extraction patterns. Make sure every token in the entity is covered by B or I tags.

If 95% of your tokens are labeled O and only 0.5% are B-invoice_number, the model will learn that predicting O is almost always correct. This is the class imbalance problem. It's normal for O to dominate, but watch for entity types that appear in fewer than 1% of tokens -- those need more training examples or the model won't learn them reliably.

Before you start training, run these checks on your labeled data. Catching errors here is cheaper than debugging a confused model later.

An I-vendor_name tag should never appear without a preceding B-vendor_name. If it does, either the B tag was missed or the entity boundaries are wrong.

def validate_bio_sequences(labels):     errors = []     for i, label in enumerate(labels):         if label.startswith('I-'):             entity_type = label[2:]             if i == 0:                 errors.append(f'I-{entity_type} at position 0 with no preceding B tag')             else:                 prev = labels[i - 1]                 if prev != f'B-{entity_type}' and prev != f'I-{entity_type}':                     errors.append(f'I-{entity_type} at position {i} follows {prev}')     return errors

Count how many times each label appears across your dataset. Flag any entity type that appears in fewer than 1% of total labels -- it likely needs more training examples.

from collections import Counter def check_distribution(all_labels):     counts = Counter(all_labels)     total = sum(counts.values())     for label, count in counts.most_common():         pct = 100 count / total *        flag = ' ** RARE **' if pct < 1.0 and label != 'O' else ''         print(f'  {label}: {count} ({pct:.1f}%){flag}')

Coordinates should fall within the page dimensions, and no bounding box should have zero or negative width or height:

def validate_bboxes(words, page_w=1000, page_h=1000):     issues = []     for w in words:         x0, y0, x1, y1 = w['bbox']         if x1 <= x0 or y1 <= y0:             issues.append(f"Zero/negative size: {w['text']}")         if x0 < 0 or y0 < 0 or x1 > page_w or y1 > page_h:             issues.append(f"Out of bounds: {w['text']}")     return issues

This depends on your document diversity and label count, but here are practical benchmarks based on fine-tuning LayoutLMv3 from a pre-trained checkpoint:

Single layout (e.g., one vendor's invoices)

These numbers assume fine-tuning from a pre-trained checkpoint, not training from scratch. Starting with a checkpoint like LayoutLMv3-base gives you document layout understanding for free -- your BIO-tagged data just teaches it which fields to extract from your specific document types.

Start small. Label 50 documents, train a model, check the error patterns, fix your taxonomy if needed, then scale up. Labeling 1,000 documents with a flawed taxonomy wastes more time than labeling 50 documents twice.

Once your data is tagged and validated, the training pipeline is straightforward. Here's the outline using Hugging Face Transformers:

from transformers import (     LayoutLMv3ForTokenClassification,     LayoutLMv3Processor,     TrainingArguments,     Trainer ) \

model = LayoutLMv3ForTokenClassification.from_pretrained(     'microsoft/layoutlmv3-base',     num_labels=len(label_list)  # your BIO labels ) \

args = TrainingArguments(     output_dir='./results',     num_train_epochs=30,     per_device_train_batch_size=4,     learning_rate=5e-5,     evaluation_strategy='epoch',     save_strategy='epoch',     load_best_model_at_end=True ) \

trainer = Trainer(     model=model,     args=args,     train_dataset=train_data,     eval_dataset=val_data ) trainer.train()

The key hyperparameters to tune are learning rate (start with 5e-5), batch size (4 or 8 depending on GPU memory), and number of epochs (30 is a good starting point for small datasets). Use an 80/10/10 train/validation/test split, and make sure document types are distributed across all three splits.

● BIO tagging assigns B (beginning), I (inside), or O (outside) to every token. It's the standard labeling scheme for token classification in document AI.

● Pair BIO labels with normalized bounding box coordinates so models learn both textual and spatial patterns.

● Design your label taxonomy before you start annotating. One label per semantic meaning, hierarchical names, and a catch-all O label.

● Validate early. Check BIO sequence integrity, label distribution, and bounding box coordinates before training.

● Start with 50-100 labeled documents, train a model, check errors, and scale up. Quality matters more than quantity.

● Use pre-labeling (regex or model-based) to speed up annotation. Manual labeling from scratch doesn't scale.

The annotation step is the foundation of every document AI pipeline. The model you train is only as good as the BIO labels you feed it. Take the time to build a clean taxonomy, label consistently, and validate before training -- it saves debugging time later and produces models that actually work in production.

#10198. Hands-on: Sony’s Walkman-revival USB-C headphones are back in stock and worth a look

Sony’s iconic Walkman headphones are back in the USB-C MDR-3R and, after quickly selling out on launch day, they’ve now been restocked. And, after using them, I can see why they sold out so quickly.

The MDR-3R are Sony’s revival of the wired over-ear headphones that many used with a classic Walkman player. The retro style still looks great in 2026, but using an original pair is a bit of a headache in today’s age of headphone jack-less smartphones. So it was nice to see Sony give this a revival with USB-C.

Update 11/24: After we first published our hands-on with MDR-3R last week, Sony’s USB-C headphones sold out pretty quickly again. As of today, though, they’re back in stock and still shipping in early December – don’t miss out!

While the style won’t be for everyone, I think these work brilliantly. The sound quality is excellent and having a pair of headphones that doesn’t fully enclose my ears is pretty refreshing. There’s no noise cancellation, but with open-ear earbuds becoming more popular, I actually really like these as a similar solution (though high volumes will definitely be heard by those around you). I’m not fully cut off from the world around me, but I’m also able to get good sound quality. Lows are the weakness here, with unimpressive bass drops, but the sound quality is crisp and clear and pretty neutral, and the open-ish style makes it feel like the room around me is filled with music.

The physical hardware is great. While a plastic and metal build is objectively not as comfy as a premium pair of ANC headphones, the extremely light weight helps MDR-3R become an afterthought. You simply forget you’re wearing them after a few minutes.

The earpads are swappable, with orange and blue options included in the box.

As for the USB-C cable, it’s fully plug-and-play. No app needed and it worked across every device I tested (including Pixel, which doesn’t work with all USB-C audio output). I do wish the cable was perhaps a few inches longer, but it’s long enough to wear the headphones with my phone in my front pocket. There’s a remote along the cable for play/pause and volume adjustments, though I think it’s a little too high on the cable.

For $59.99, these are a fun pair of USB-C headphones to have on hand. They sound good, work great, and don’t cost an arm and a leg. I can see why they sold out so quickly on launch day – in just 10 hours, Sony told me.

Sony MDR-3R are back in stock now and shipping in early December. If you want a pair, I say don’t wait. They’re well worth at least trying if you can get them for retail price – please don’t give in to the scalpers selling these at double, they’re not worth that.

#10191. What to get right before you ship webhooks

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, and Cursor are all sending webhooks now, and all four built them on Standard Webhooks, the open spec written by Svix. Downloads of the spec's npm package went from 1.7 million a month in September 2025 to 220 million a month currently.

This may be boring to hear for the nth time, but the reason is agents. An individual person checks on a running job a few times. An agent told to watch for changes checks every resource, every minute, for as long as it runs, and almost every one of those requests comes back with nothing new. An agent that starts a job, polls until it finishes, and then starts the next one spends most of its time waiting: six steps with 15 seconds of polling each add 90 seconds, even when every operation is fast. Polling more often adds load, and polling less often adds delay, so there's not really a sweet spot.

An event-driven API gets rid of that trade-off. It tells consumers when something changed or finished, so they stop asking. That matters most for work that runs for minutes or hours, like batch inference, fine-tuning, and video generation, and many APIs still don't offer it. Of the 57 AI APIs with long-running jobs that Svix reviewed for its State of Webhooks 2027 report, 19 make you poll for the result.

Webhooks are how most APIs push those events, and you can get a v1 out pretty fast. Making it dependable is an infrastructure problem, which is a class of problem that requires more time by default. Before you ship webhooks, you need an append-only event log behind every delivery, a fixed event format, signed payloads, retries with backoff, event IDs receivers can deduplicate on, per-endpoint isolation, and delivery logs your customers can read. If you skip one of these things, a customer is just going to bring it up later.

Each section below covers one of them with adaptable code, and Standard Webhooks works with existing verification libraries in most languages.

If the request handler that changes the data also sends the webhook, a crash between the commit and the HTTP call loses the event, and nothing records that it should have gone out. A customer whose endpoint was down for longer than your retry window has no way to find out what they missed.

Both problems go away if you write each event to an append-only table, scoped per customer, in the same transaction as the change. Webhooks are delivered from that table, and the same table backs an HTTP endpoint customers can read from a cursor. Svix calls this design POKE (Publish Once, Kickoff Everywhere). Build the log first: a consumer who misses a webhook needs somewhere to resume from.

Every code path that changes state calls one helper inside its own transaction:

Pass the helper the transaction handle. An insert through the connection pool commits separately, so you can end up with the event and no change, or the change and no event.

The advisory lock prevents a subtler bug. Postgres assigns sequence values at insert time, but rows only become visible at commit. With two concurrent writers, a reader can see ID 102, store it as its cursor, and then never see ID 101 when the slower transaction commits. Holding a per-customer lock until commit makes each customer's events visible in ID order.

Reading the log is one indexed range query. The endpoint returns a cursor even when there are no new events, so a client can always store it and ask again later:

authenticate stands in for the middleware that already protects your API and sets req.customerId. That scoping stops one customer from reading another's events. A customer who was offline for a week asks for events after their last cursor and gets everything they missed, in order. Publish your retention period, since it limits how far back that works. The same log can also serve synthetic created events for existing records when a new integration needs to load its initial state.

Customers write handlers against your payloads, so you'll be supporting every field you ship.

Give each event an id, type, timestamp, and data. Use the log row's ID as the event ID so webhooks and the pull endpoint agree. Name types as a resource plus a past-tense action, like invoice.paid, and publish a catalog with an example payload for each type.

Emit an event for each state change a consumer would otherwise poll for. Deletions are the ones teams forget most. Long-running jobs need completion and failure events, such as export.completed and export.failed.

Adding a field is safe. Removing or renaming one breaks handlers, so ship a new type such as invoice.paid.v2 and keep sending the old one until customers have moved over.

Anyone who finds a customer's webhook URL can POST a fake invoice.paid to it. A signature lets the receiver confirm the request came from you, wasn't modified in transit, and isn't an old request being replayed.

Standard Webhooks signs the string id.timestamp.body with HMAC-SHA256, using a separate secret for each endpoint. Including the timestamp is what lets receivers reject old requests. Generate secrets with "whsec_" + crypto.randomBytes(24).toString("base64").

Send the result in three headers. The ID comes from the log and stays the same on every retry. The timestamp is the time of the current attempt, so each retry gets a new signature.

Receivers need the matching check, so put it in your docs:

The function takes the raw body because frameworks that parse JSON and serialize it again change the bytes, and the signature stops matching. It compares with crypto.timingSafeEqual, since === returns faster on an early mismatch and leaks timing information. It rejects requests more than five minutes old to block replays. The signature header is a space-separated list so that during a secret rotation you can send one signature per secret and the receiver accepts either.

That code shows how the scheme works. In production, use a Standard Webhooks library on both ends. It implements the same algorithm:

Receiving endpoints go down during deploys and outages, and they return errors when they're rate limited. If you only try once, each of those is a lost webhook.

Count any non-2xx response as a failure, redirects included. Following a redirect to an arbitrary host is a security problem in its own right. Count slow responses as failures too. The code below gives up after 15 seconds. Publish whatever limit you pick, because customer handlers have to respond within it.

These delays match the schedule Svix runs in production: eight attempts over a little more than 27 hours. The early retries cover restarts. The later ones give someone time to notice an outage and fix it. After each failure, pass the number of attempts so far to nextAttemptAt, and when it returns null, mark the delivery as failed. The event is still in the log, so once the customer fixes their server they can replay it or read it from the pull endpoint.

Store attempt state in a table next to the log. Timers in process memory disappear when the process restarts, and you don't need a message broker to avoid that.

Decide what happens to endpoints that never come back. Svix disables an endpoint after five days of continuous failure and emits an event so the sender can tell the customer. Pick your own threshold, notify the customer when you disable their endpoint, and let them turn it back on.

Retries mean some events arrive twice. Usually the receiver processed the event but timed out before you saw its response, so you sent it again. Senders accept this at-least-once behavior because the alternative is dropping events, and receivers handle the duplicates.

Receivers deduplicate on webhook-id, which doesn't change between retries. The insert that stores the event can also be the duplicate check:

webhook_inbox needs a text primary key id and a jsonb column payload. The handler verifies the signature, stores the event, and returns 204. A background worker processes the inbox, so the handler responds well within the sender's timeout. express.raw gives the route the unparsed body that verification needs.

Put this pattern in your docs with a note about ordering. Events in the log have a fixed order, but webhook deliveries don't always arrive in it. A retried invoice.created can land after invoice.paid. Receivers should keep whichever event has the newer updated_at, or read the pull endpoint when they need strict ordering.

With a shared worker pool, one customer's hanging endpoint can tie up every worker for the full timeout while other customers' events wait. Limit concurrent deliveries per endpoint, and slow down delivery to endpoints that keep failing.

Customer-supplied URLs are also an attack surface. Someone can register http://169.254.169.254/ or an internal hostname and get your servers to call your cloud metadata service or private network. This is server-side request forgery (SSRF). Resolve the hostname before connecting, reject private and loopback addresses, and don't follow redirects. The delivery code sets redirect: "manual" for this reason.

Without a self-serve view, every "did you send it?" question goes to an engineer who has to search logs.

Give customers a page to add and remove endpoints, choose event types, and rotate secrets. For each message, show every attempt with its timestamp, status code, and response body. The response body usually contains the customer's own error message. Add buttons to replay a failed message and to send a test event.

Expose the same operations in your API. Creating an endpoint and choosing its event types should take one authenticated request, because scripts and AI agents now set up a lot of integrations and can't click through a dashboard.

This is the biggest item here because it needs a UI, and it's the one teams most often leave out of the first release.

You can build all of this yourself, and many teams have. It comes down to the fact that it's cheap to manage delivery for 99% of your webhooks, but it's nontrivial to guarantee it for 100% of them. This is also ignoring the UX side such as offering the customer portal for your customers to manage their webhooks in.

Svix, which sponsored this article, handles webhook delivery. You keep the event log and decide what goes in it. A small relay reads new rows and passes each one to Svix:

Svix also hosts the debugging portal. This call returns a single-use link where the customer can manage endpoints, read delivery logs, and replay messages:

The free tier includes 50,000 messages a month and doesn't require a credit card.

Before the first customer registers an endpoint, you should be able to answer yes to each of these:

#10189. Google Maps update on Android Auto adds navigation routes for motorcycles

Google Maps is rolling out an update on Android Auto that provides better navigation routes specifically for motorcycles.

Users in India have recently noticed that Google Maps for Android Auto is showing a new message asking them to restart the app to “switch to two-wheeler directions.” Once enabled, a motorcycle icon appears on the ETA card.

On a support page, Google details this mode, explaining that the mode tailors directions to skip routes where two-wheelers are not allowed, while also included narrow roads that may be faster. Google says that the feature is only available in “limited locations.”

These directions show you routes away from roads where two-wheelers aren’t allowed. You may be directed towards shortcuts along narrower side roads.

The page also explains that it is only available for mobile devices, with no mention of Android Auto and CarPlay. Yet, a number of users are seeing the adjustment on their devices within the past two weeks, so it seems Google is expanding it out to Android Auto. That makes sense, as Android Auto has been working to better accommodate motorcycles for a while, and some motorcycles even now have Android Auto support built-in.

#10188. Securing Your Agents: A Hands-On Guide to Agent Anomaly Detection

As highlighted in our announcement post, we said that each new model generation lets agents do more on their own. However, the more an agent can reach, the more one bad decision can cost. You've probably seen a few of these in the news lately:

Because these events produced no system crashes or logging errors, the teams behind these agents had no way to know until the damage occurred.

Agent Anomaly Detection on the Gemini Platform watches for exactly these issues. It reads the traces and logs your agents already produce and flags sessions where an agent goes outside the job it was built for.

Before you point it at your production agents, you'll probably want to see it work in a test environment. In this hands-on guide, we’ll walk you step-by-step through setting up Agent Anomaly Detection and using it to capture your very first agent behavior anomaly. By the end of this post, you'll have:

To get started, open the companion notebook Detect anomalous agent behavior with Agent Anomaly Detection in a separate tab (available on Google Colab or GitHub). As you read through this guide, it will prompt you when to execute notebook cells or interact directly with the Google Cloud console.

If a step can be done in the UI console, this post shows that path, with screenshots. The notebook does the same thing through the Agent Anomaly Detection API, so by the end you'll also have the code to automate enablement and pull findings into your own tooling.

2. A place to run the notebook: Google Colab, Colab Enterprise, or a local Jupyter environment. The notebook installs the latest ADK for Python itself. Agent Anomaly Detection is compatible with agents running ADK 1.2+, though ADK 2.0+ is preferred for best results.

3. A Cloud Logging bucket and an Observability dataset in either US or EU multi-region. (Both resources need to be in the same multi-region)

4. In the notebook: run the Get started cells. They install the packages, authenticate you, take your project ID, and turn on the four APIs that Agent Anomaly Detection needs.

First, let’s create a Customer Support Agent called “anomaly-detection-refund-demo”. Our example agent is responsible for helping customers check their order history and request refunds. However, it has a deliberate flaw: the agent has access to an overprivileged fetch_records tool which can read any table, including a `payroll` table containing sensitive employee data. All three parts of the lethal trifecta are here: the agent can read private data, it takes input from people it can't vet, and it sends what it finds back in its reply. Tool permissions and guardrails should stop most of this, but they only block what someone thought to write down. Agent Anomaly Detection flags the sessions that get past them, usually within a few minutes.

The notebook has the full agent. The part that matters is the tool, which never checks the table name against an allowlist:

All data is canned and in memory, so nothing real is read or refunded. This tool is intended to be over-permissive to illustrate AAD functionality. Don't copy this tool into production.

The Step 1 cells deploy the agent to Agent Runtime. Two details in the deploy cell are what make the agent visible to Agent Anomaly Detection. First, it sets three environment variables on the deployment, which turn on telemetry and write prompt and response content to Cloud Logging, where detection looks for it:

Second, it sends the agent one test message right after deploying. Agent Anomaly Detection discovers agents through their telemetry, so an agent that has never served a request does not appear under Monitored agents.

The deployment takes about 5 to 10 minutes and prints the agent's resource name and the reply to the test message:

For more information on how to create and deploy an agent, take a look at these guides: Creating an Agent and Deploying an Agent.

In the console: The sample code we provide already enables logging and telemetry when deploying the agent. Verify this by navigating to the Agent Deployments page (Gemini Platform > Agents > Deployments) and clicking “Configure” next to the recently created anomaly-detection-refund-demo agent to open the Observability Service Configuration.

Great! You now have a working agent that is successfully configured and ready to be monitored by Agent Anomaly Detection.

Now let’s set up Agent Anomaly Detection to monitor your agent’s traffic.

In the console: navigate to the Agent Platform > Agents > Audit page and click “Enable”.

Choose your agent’s Logging Bucket and Observability Dataset, making sure they are in the same region (us-multi-region or eu-multi-region) enable “Automatically monitor new agents”, then click “Agree and continue” and Agent Anomaly Detection will be set up in your project.

If you'd rather do this in code, the first Step 2 cell in the notebook creates the same configuration through the API, using the default _Default bucket and _Trace dataset with automatic monitoring on.

Setup takes around 30 minutes. Once it’s done loading you’re ready to proceed to the next step! Because you enabled automatic monitoring and the agent already served a request in Step 1, setup picks it up right away. Check that it shows as Active under Settings > Monitored agents before you continue; sessions that start earlier aren't analyzed.

Now that the agent is being monitored, let's see Agent Anomaly Detection in action! The Step 3 cells send one normal conversation and one anomalous conversation, each in its own session. The normal conversation is a customer listing their orders and getting a refund for a broken lamp. The anomalous conversation simulates a user who finds the fetch_records flaw and uses it to pull payroll data:

Notice that the user brings almost nothing: a customer ID and one first name. Asking for a table that doesn't exist makes the agent reveal the existing tables, which is how they learn there is a payroll table within reach.

The notebook prints both transcripts with the agent's tool calls, and the session IDs you'll look up next.

Anomaly Detection will scan the sessions within 5 minutes. In the landing page, you should see that a couple sessions have been processed and one anomalous session detected.

Let’s take a look at the anomalous session in more detail. Click the session id to open the detail view or paste the anomalous session id into the search bar.

In our run, the top finding was Tool misuse at High severity, with 95% probability. Your detectors and severities may differ, because the models behind the analysis are non-deterministic. The recommended solution is to restrict the `fetch_records` tool to read from specific tables.

Click an invocation in the Session pane to see its analysis in Details. Each invocation is Medium on its own, but together they make the session High.

To stay informed without watching the console, connect Agent Anomaly Detection to your existing alerting workflows.

In Cloud Monitoring: Agent Anomaly Detection publishes two metrics: Detected events, which counts anomalies by detector and severity, and Monitored agents, which tracks your detection coverage. To view these metrics, click the arrow next to "View in Cloud Monitoring" on the Anomaly detection page and select a metric to open it in Metrics Explorer.

To be notified of new anomalies, go to Monitoring > Alerting, click "Create policy", and select the Detected events metric. Leave the filters empty so the alert covers every anomaly, including the demo's, then set the rolling window to 5 minutes with the delta function, and set the threshold to above 0. Once you know what's normal for your agents, add a severity filter (for example High or Critical) so you're only alerted on the findings that matter. A metric-absence policy on Monitored agents will also alert you if monitoring stops.

In Security Command Center: Each anomaly is also published as an AGENT_SESSION_ANOMALY finding in the Security Command Center if it is enabled for your organization. Click "View in Security Command Center" to triage these findings alongside your other threats, use "Set security marks" to tag and track them, and set up notifications for high-severity findings.

You’ve now seen Agent Anomaly Detection in action: it successfully flagged the session where our agent went off-script to read payroll records, while evaluating the benign session, and correctly not flagging an anomaly. The finding told you why it was flagged and how to fix the underlying issue, and alerts in Cloud Monitoring and Security Command Center ensure you are notified of future anomalies.

When you're ready to secure your own production workloads, follow this rollout plan:

To review the complete list of prerequisites or to request Private Preview access for your project, head over to the Agent Anomaly Detection documentation.

#10179. How to Add AI Facial Expression Editing to Your Video Pipeline (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Product teams keep asking engineers for the same feature: “Can users fix the reaction in this clip without reshooting it?” It lands on the backlog as “add expression editing,” and it hides a lot of decisions.

This guide compares the realistic integration options in 2026, sketches a pipeline architecture, models costs, includes a small runnable job helper, and covers the consent and audit logging you need before changing a real person’s face. Vendor details change quickly, so check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Information about features, pricing, APIs and policies in this article was checked on November 10, 2026 and may change. Check each vendor’s documentation before you build against it.

Performance transfer is great for animation, but someone has to record a new take for every change. If your promise is “fix the reaction in the footage you already have,” direct editing fits better.

DRAMA is an AI facial changer from SyncMonster. In the web app, users upload a video or image (MP4, MOV, JPG, PNG), pick a face, choose an emotion, and set intensity on a slider from subtle to strong. The app offers four core emotions (Happy, Sad, Angry, Excited) plus presets such as Surprised, Serious, Evil and Intimidating, with the extra presets on paid plans. It supports emotion blending and editing one face in a group shot without touching the others. SyncMonster states that edits keep the original resolution, lighting and details such as glasses and beards, which is a vendor claim you should test on your own footage.

For developers, SyncMonster publishes public API documentation for DRAMA. Here is what those docs state, as checked on November 10, 2026:

A few things to note honestly. The same generate endpoint also serves other SyncMonster features behind their own flags, so read the parameter table carefully. In the version we checked, the parameter table marks an audio input as required on generate, and the drama-only example includes one, so plan for that in your request builder. The emotion values exposed in the API are the four core emotions, and the extra app presets did not appear in the API docs. The docs describe polling only, with no webhooks mentioned, and they do not publish rate limits, an SDK, or a numeric maximum video duration (an error code exists for exceeding a duration limit).

Runway exposes Act-Two through its developer API as the character_performance endpoint. You send a character (image or video) and a driving performance video, and Act-Two transfers facial expression, and optionally body and hand motion, onto the character. Output is 24fps.

Runway’s API pricing guide lists Act-Two at 5 credits per second of output, with credits priced at $0.01 each, so $0.05 per second. Earlier documentation mentioned a 15-credit minimum per generation, and the current pricing page does not list one, so check for minimums before you budget short clips. The documentation also exposes an expression intensity setting to control how strongly the performance is applied.

The catch is the input requirement: every edit needs someone to act the new reaction. That suits character animation, but it is a heavy ask for a user who wants a slightly warmer smile in an interview clip. It also drives one character per generation.

LivePortrait is an open-source portrait animation framework from Kuaishou’s KwaiVGI group. It animates a source image or video using a driving video or a saved motion template, supports video-to-video portrait editing, and offers retargeting controls for eyes and lips.

The README sets up a Python 3.10 conda environment and expects Git and FFmpeg, with an NVIDIA GPU recommended. It also notes that Apple Silicon runs roughly 20x slower than an RTX 4090, and that Humans mode needs PYTORCH_ENABLE_MPS_FALLBACK=1 on a Mac. That matters if you were hoping to prototype on a MacBook and ship the same setup. A basic run adapted from the README looks like this:

Here -s is the source portrait (an image or video) and -d is the driving clip. Retargeting is exposed through additional options and the Gradio interface, so check the README for current names because they change between releases. Also read the repository’s license file before you plan any commercial use.

The Hugging Face Spaces demo is handy for experiments, but production means owning GPU provisioning, queueing, model updates and quality review. You still steer with a driving input rather than a “happier by 30%” control.

Rule of thumb: if expression editing is a feature rather than your product, buy. If you need on-prem processing or model-level changes, build on LivePortrait and budget for ops.

Whatever the backend, the pipeline has five stages.

Here is the shape of the worker loop. This is illustrative pseudocode only, not any vendor’s SDK or real endpoints:

The idempotency and backoff tips below are easy to get wrong, so here is a small vendor-neutral module. The key function hashes the input file together with the parameters, so a retry with the same input maps to the same job. The poller backs off exponentially up to a ceiling and gives up after a maximum wait. It uses only the Python standard library.

A short test confirms the behavior: the same input gives the same key regardless of parameter order, different parameters give a different key, and the poller returns once the state becomes done.

Credits are not a shared currency across vendors, so compare units first and money second.

For prototyping, the DRAMA web app has a free plan (60 credits, 1080p, up to 2 minutes of processing, 2 projects, watermarked output), which is enough to judge quality on your own sample footage before writing integration code. The docs we checked list API billing in credits but do not state a dollar price per API credit, so confirm that with SyncMonster before you finalize a pricing model. Do not assume web-app plan credits convert at the same rate as API credits.

Plan details are as listed by the vendor on November 10, 2026 and can change.

Changing a real person’s expression can change what viewers believe they felt, so treat this as a product requirement.

For a product feature that edits reactions in existing footage, the DRAMA API is the most direct hosted option among those compared here: face detection with thumbnails for selection, per-face edits, and emotion values you set in code, with no driving performance. Go in knowing its current limits: four emotion parameters in the API, polling instead of webhooks, and no published rate limits or SDK. Starting with the free plan on your own clips is a low-risk way to judge quality. Product details and API access for this AI expression changer are on SyncMonster’s site.

Choose the Runway API if your users want to animate characters from an acted performance. Choose LivePortrait if you need on-prem processing or model-level control and have the GPU and ops capacity. Leave Flawless DeepEditor to studio editorial teams, and Photoshop to still-image workflows.

Yes. DRAMA by SyncMonster has a hosted REST API for editing expressions in existing video using emotion values, and Runway offers Act-Two through its API for performance transfer. LivePortrait is open source and can be wrapped in your own service.

Yes, with a direct editing tool. The DRAMA API takes emotion values from 0 to 1 for happy, sad, excited and angry on a selected face. Runway Act-Two and LivePortrait both expect a driving performance or motion template.

Run face detection first, let the user pick a face, then send that face’s ID with the edit request. The DRAMA API returns a face ID and a thumbnail for each detected face for this purpose.

Per vendor docs as of November 10, 2026: the DRAMA API bills 0.5 credit per second for face detection and 1 credit per second for drama generation, while Runway’s Act-Two API bills 5 credits per second at $0.01 per credit. LivePortrait costs whatever your GPU time costs.

Yes, but its README notes Apple Silicon can be about 20x slower than an RTX 4090. An NVIDIA GPU is recommended for anything beyond experiments.

Not a public one. DeepEditor is an Avid Media Composer extension with access by application, aimed at film and TV editorial teams.

Log the requester, consent record, selected face, emotion and intensity, backend used, input and output hashes, credits used, and a timestamp, and keep the original file.

#10170. Android Auto update improves support for wide displays, but not everyone will see it [Gallery]

Google has rolled out a new update for Android Auto that improves support for wider display formats, but there’s a good chance you won’t be able to see it.

Android Auto has to scale to a lot of different display shapes and sizes, and its functionality varies depending on what you’re using as the platform adapts to what your display can handle. This can sometimes mean you are locked out of a specific feature or need to access it another way. A couple of infamous examples of this include the “Report” option in Google Maps being hidden on smaller displays, as well as the weather card only being on larger, taller displays.

That said, Google is now making an effort to improve how apps on Android Auto adapters to wider formats.

In the latest app update (v18.2), Android Auto is tweaking apps to switch from a top-bar layout to a sidebar for navigation. So, in Spotify, this means the “Home,” “Recents,” “Browse,” and “Library” shortcuts move to a sidebar rather than being up top.

It’s a good use of space, as it allows more content to be visible on wide but short displays. Android Authority spotted the change across Spotify, YouTube Music, and Phone, implying this is probably a platform-level change rather than being tied to individual app updates.

It’s a great change, but one you might not be able to see.

As mentioned, Android Auto adjusts to take advantage of whatever display space it has available, and that often includes putting Google Maps off to the side of other apps. While you can easily set Maps to take up the full display, other apps are less likely to be able to do that, with Maps semi-permanently off to the opposite side. In a car with a wide display we tested for this new format, Maps prevented any other apps from going wide enough to show the new layout, so your results may vary. In many cases, this new layout may simply not be visible. In a way, though, that’s a good thing – it shows that Google is addressing even the most niche aspects of Android Auto, and that’s a win for everyone.

#10169. Your Pose Model Gives You Coordinates, Not Meaning

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Pose detection in the browser is a solved problem. You can load a model, point it at a webcam, and get a skeleton tracking a human body in real time, on a laptop, with no server involved.

And then you have a skeleton that follows someone around the screen, which is a toy.

The gap between that toy and a feature someone would pay for is smaller than most developers expect, and it isn't made of machine learning. It's made of trigonometry you already know. This article builds a working bicep-curl rep counter to show exactly where that line falls — and more importantly, which side of it your bugs live on.

I'm using MediaPipe Pose Landmarker from Google AI Edge, which is Apache-2.0 licensed and runs entirely in the tab. Here's the whole setup:

Point that at a

Thirty-three points. Each one is a normalised coordinate — x and y run 0 to 1 across the frame, z is a relative depth estimate — plus a visibility score saying how confident the model is that it can actually see that joint.

That's the entire output. Coordinates and confidences.

The model has no concept of exercise. It doesn't know what an arm is for. It has never heard of a bicep curl. It reports where it thinks your joints are, thirty-odd times a second, and stops.

Everything that turns that into a product is yours, and it's ordinary code.

A bicep curl is a change in the angle at the elbow. The elbow angle is defined by three of those 33 points: shoulder, elbow, wrist. In MediaPipe's pose topology those are indices 11, 13 and 15 on the left side, 12, 14 and 16 on the right.

Getting the angle is two vectors and a dot product:

This is the same maths you'd use to point a game character in a direction. Nothing about it is machine learning, and that matters for a reason I'll come back to.

One thing to notice: I'm ignoring z entirely. The depth estimate is relative, not metric — it's good enough for working out which parts of a body are nearer the camera, and not good enough to measure anything. For a 2D elbow angle, using it adds noise and buys nothing.

Here's where almost everyone's first attempt breaks.

The naive version looks at the angle and counts whenever the arm is up:

Run that at 30 frames a second and one slow curl gives you about twenty reps. You haven't counted repetitions. You've counted frames in which the arm happened to be bent.

A rep isn't a state. It's a transition between states:

Fifteen lines, and that's the feature. The counter increments exactly once per cycle, on the edge, because the guard on phase makes every other frame in that cycle a no-op.

If you used a single threshold — say, 100 degrees — you'd get a counter that fires repeatedly whenever someone holds their arm near 100 degrees and the estimate jitters by a degree either side. Every tiny fluctuation across the line is a transition, and every transition is a rep.

Two widely separated thresholds give you hysteresis. Once the arm is "up," it has to travel all the way past 150 degrees before the counter will consider another rep. Noise of a few degrees can't produce a count because no amount of jitter near 55 crosses 150.

This is the same trick a thermostat uses to avoid switching the heating on and off every few seconds, and it's the single most useful pattern in this whole category of work. Any time you turn a continuous signal into discrete events, you want two thresholds, not one.

visibility is the field people skip, and skipping it is how you get phantom reps.

When a joint goes out of frame or gets occluded, the model still returns a coordinate for it. It is obliged to return 33 points. What it does is return a guess, with a low visibility score attached — and a guessed wrist position can swing wildly between frames, which looks exactly like a very fast curl.

Returning null and having update bail out means an occluded frame holds the current state rather than corrupting it. The arm stays "up" until the model can actually see it come down.

If you want to be stricter, smooth the angle over the last three or four frames with a rolling median before it reaches the state machine. A median rejects single-frame outliers in a way a mean doesn't.

Once you've written one of these, you start recognising the shape everywhere:

None of those require a different model. They're the same 33 coordinates with different geometry sitting on top, and the geometry is a few dozen lines each.

Here's why I keep insisting on the distinction between coordinates and meaning.

When my rep counter miscounts, I don't retrain anything. I don't collect a dataset, I don't label examples, I don't spin up a training job. I look at two things: my thresholds, and my state machine. One of them is wrong.

That's a normal bug in normal code, and you already know how to find it. You can log the angle. You can set a breakpoint on the transition. You can write a unit test that feeds a synthetic sequence of angles through update and asserts the count — no camera, no model, no fixtures:

The hard part of this problem — turning pixels into joint positions — was solved by a research team and shipped to you as a file you can download. The part that's left is the part you're already good at.

You are not blocked on machine learning knowledge. You were blocked on nobody telling you this was available.

I set delegate: 'GPU' above without comment, and it deserves one. MediaPipe Tasks lets you choose between a CPU path and a GPU path, and availability of the GPU path varies by browser, platform and graphics driver.

The practical consequence is that you have to test the CPU path, because for some meaningful share of your users it isn't a fallback — it's the only path they will ever take. If you develop on a machine where the GPU delegate works and never exercise the CPU one, you have shipped something you've never run.

That, and the question of how often you should run the model at all, is a bigger subject than this article. It's the next one.

Jay Nirmal is a Senior Software Engineer at Meazure Learning with over eight years of experience in distributed systems, cloud infrastructure, WebRTC, and production reliability. He leads engineering initiatives supporting large-scale digital assessment platforms, focusing on high-availability systems, real-time media infrastructure, recording reliability, and assessment integrity. Jay holds a Master of Science in Information Systems from Northeastern University and a Bachelor of Engineering in Computer Engineering from the University of Mumbai.

#10167. WhatsApp officially adds scheduled messages, and the rollout is scheduled too

It’s about time. WhatsApp announced today that it is finally adding support for scheduled messages in its app.

A staple of many other messaging apps, WhatsApp is adding scheduled messages to its app. This gives users the ability to write a message but schedule sending it to another user or group chat at a later time.

Scheduled messages on WhatsApp can be sent up to 14 days after you draft them in both 1:1 chats and group chats. You can schedule through a long-press on the send button, with a menu popping up to let you pick the future time when the message will be sent. You can also see scheduled messages before they’re sent to cancel sending or edit the message.

WhatsApp notes that these messages are sent even if your phone’s battery is dead or if the device is off, and everything is still end-to-end encrypted. It works with text, photos, and videos, but apparently not with voice notes.

That said, the rollout is also scheduled out. WhatsApp says the feature will start showing up for users “over the coming weeks” globally, so you might be waiting a bit longer for this long overdue release.

#10161. How to Build Conference Talk Slides With AI Without Looking Generic

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Conference talks fail on slides more often than on content. The speaker knows the material and has something worth saying, but the deck is a wall of bullets, or a template everyone in the room has seen before, or forty slides for a twenty-minute slot. AI presentation makers can now produce a complete deck from an outline in minutes, which solves the time problem and creates a new one: the output tends toward exactly the uniform, stock-image, bullet-heavy style that audiences have learned to tune out.

This article explains how conference slides differ from other decks, why AI-generated decks look generic and what specifically causes it, a workflow that uses an AI presentation maker for speed while keeping the talk distinctive, how to handle the slide types that matter most in a talk (the opening, the demonstration, the data, the close), and a checklist to run before the talk.

A conference talk is spoken, timed and performed to a room. That changes the rules compared with a reading deck or a business presentation.

The causes are specific and therefore fixable.

Template uniformity. Every slide uses the same layout family, so the deck has no visual rhythm. Audiences read the sameness as mass-produced.

Bullet default. Given a paragraph, the tool produces three to five bullets. Given twenty paragraphs, twenty bullet slides.

Stock imagery. Icons and photos chosen by keyword match the topic superficially and add no meaning. A talk about database indexing gets a picture of a filing cabinet.

Label titles. “Introduction”, “Background”, “Key Challenges”, “Conclusion”. Titles that name the category rather than make the point.

Flattened emphasis. The tool does not know which of your ideas is the one the audience should leave with, so every slide gets equal weight.

No pacing. Every slide carries the same amount of content, so there is no build, no pause, no payoff.

Each of these is a default that can be overridden, either in how the input is prepared or in a short editing pass afterwards.

Step 1: Write the talk before the slides. Not a script necessarily, but the argument: what the audience knows at the start, what they know at the end, and the four to six moves that get them there. Write the one sentence you want them to repeat to a colleague afterwards. If this is not clear, no deck will fix it.

Step 2: Write the slide titles as the things you will say. Each title is a sentence, in your voice, that states the point of that moment in the talk. “Indexes make reads fast by making writes slower” is a title. “Index trade-offs” is not. Read the titles in order; they should be the talk in miniature.

Step 3: Mark the pacing. Next to each title, note whether the slide is a hold (a key point, stays up while you talk for a minute or more), a beat (a quick slide in a sequence), or a reveal (something shown, a demo, a result). This tells you where to put effort.

Step 4: Generate. Paste the titles with one or two sentences of content under each into an ai presentation maker. Ask for a slide count close to your title count. Pick the plainest theme available; distinctiveness will come from your content, not the template.

Step 5: Strip the bullets. On every hold slide, delete the bullets and leave the title, or the title plus one image or one number. On beat slides, keep at most one short line. The content that was in the bullets goes into speaker notes, which is where it belonged.

Step 6: Replace every stock image. Delete icons. Replace keyword-matched photos with your own material: a screenshot, a diagram you drew, a chart from your data, a photograph you took, a single large word. If a slide has no genuine visual, a title alone on a plain background is better than a filler image.

Step 7: Build the reveals by hand. Demos, before-and-after comparisons, the key chart. These are the slides the audience will remember, and they need more care than a generator can give. See the section below.

Step 8: Break the layout rhythm deliberately. Make the two or three most important slides look different: full-bleed image, a single enormous number, a black slide with one line of white text. Contrast is what makes a moment land.

Step 9: Rehearse with a timer. The slide count will be wrong in the first rehearsal. Cut, merge or split until the pacing matches the time.

The opening. Not a title slide with your name and a logo while you say hello. Open on something that earns attention: a surprising number, a question, a screenshot of the problem, a single provocative sentence. Your name and affiliation can come on slide two, or be said rather than shown.

The problem statement. One slide, one sentence, stays up while you explain why the audience should care. No bullets.

The demonstration. If the talk has a demo, record it. Live demos fail in rooms with unreliable connections and unfamiliar projectors. A recorded demo with the audio off, narrated live, gives the same effect with none of the risk. If you must go live, have the recording ready as a fallback.

The data. One chart per slide, one message per chart, the message in the title. Strip gridlines, legends and secondary series. Highlight the one line or bar that matters in colour and grey out the rest. Label directly. Type large enough to read from the back.

The “so what.” After the evidence, a slide that states what it means in one sentence. This is a hold slide; give it time.

The close. The one sentence you want them to repeat, alone on the final slide. Not “Thank you” or “Questions?”; those can be said. The last thing on screen while you take questions should be your point.

Because the slides are now sparse, two things need handling.

First, the speaker notes must carry what you intend to say, in cue form: the key phrases, the number you must not forget, the transition to the next slide. The generator will have put the stripped bullet content there; edit it into cues rather than prose.

Second, the shared deck. Attendees who download a sparse deck afterwards get little from it. Options: export with notes visible; publish a companion blog post or document; or build a second “reading” version with more text on each slide. Many speakers do the first and link to a post.

For a twenty-minute talk, fifteen to twenty-five slides is typical once hold and beat slides are counted. For a forty-five-minute talk, thirty to fifty. Build-up sequences of several quick beats inflate the count without inflating the time, which is fine. What is not fine is a deck of forty dense slides for twenty minutes; that is a talk that will run over and leave the audience behind.

Rehearse at least twice with a timer, once alone and once with a listener. Cut the content you rush. Nobody has ever complained that a talk finished two minutes early.

Lightning talks (five to ten minutes). Five to eight slides, almost all holds. One idea, one piece of evidence, one takeaway. Cut everything else.

Workshops and tutorials. More slides, more text, because attendees will refer back and follow along. The reading-deck rules apply more here; still, keep each step to one slide.

Panel contributions. Often no slides at all, or two or three for a specific point. Resist bringing a full deck.

Keynotes. Fewer slides, longer holds, more full-bleed images and single sentences. The speaker carries almost everything; the slides punctuate.

Remote talks. The screen is small and the audience is distracted. Larger type still, more frequent slide changes to hold attention, and a recorded demo is essential because screen-sharing a live one adds another failure point.

How much of the deck should the AI tool produce? The structural first draft: layouts, consistency, speaker notes populated. Expect to edit every slide and rebuild the key ones.

Is it acceptable to have slides with only a title? Yes, and it is often the strongest format for a spoken talk. The audience listens instead of reading.

What about code on slides? Short excerpts only, in a large monospace type, with the relevant lines highlighted and the rest dimmed. Anything longer belongs in the companion post.

Should I use animations? Simple builds (revealing elements in sequence) help pacing. Transitions and effects distract. Keep to the former.

How do I make the deck distinctive if I cannot design? Your material is what makes it distinctive: your screenshots, your data, your diagrams, your sentences. Design restraint plus real content beats decorated templates every time.

#10160. Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. Last week, we introduced Gemini 4 Hydrogen, which brings strong spatial and embodied reasoning to a much faster model built for speed and efficiency.

To help developers put Hydrogen to work, today we're launching the Robotics plugin on Google Antigravity: a set of skills for hardware onboarding, simulation and robot control turning Antigravity into a one-stop robotics workbench. Together, they enable a developer to go from a robot in a box to one that can perform a working application in an afternoon. To install the plugin, open Antigravity, go to Customizations and search for Robotics on the Build With Google shelf.

Gemini 4 Argon and Carbon showed that a general model can drive a robot arm directly, and Gemini 4 Hydrogen preserves that ability. Give it a goal in plain language and a camera view, and it plans the task, writes and runs the control code, and keeps adjusting until the job is done. On the Artificial Analysis Robotics 0.1 benchmark, Gemini 4 Hydrogen controls a real 6-axis arm through eight everyday tabletop tasks, from pouring a drink to packing blocks into a box. Hydrogen scores 58, similar to Argon at a lower cost per token.

Hydrogen brings the specialized skills of our Gemini Robotics ER 2 (Embodied Reasoning) models into the core Gemini 4 model series, and it beats them on nearly every axis. On the public ERQA benchmark, Hydrogen outperforms Gemini Robotics ER 2 on spatial reasoning tasks such as multi-view correspondence and 3D trajectory reasoning. For developers using Gemini Robotics ER 2 today, we recommend trying out Gemini 4 Hydrogen for these workloads.

A fast, capable model is only half the story. Building a robot application has always meant gluing together drivers, calibration scripts, simulators, perception code, and a UI, which requires robotics expertise and a lot of time. The new Robotics plugin for Google Antigravity packages that work into skills Gemini can use on your behalf:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Here's what that looks like end to end: set up a robot and build real applications.

Starting from an empty desk, a developer plugs an I2RT YAM arm and an external depth camera into a laptop and asks Hydrogen in Antigravity to bring it up. The plugin's Skills install the software, find the camera, calibrate the arm, and run test motions. The arm is calibrated and ready for tasks in about 10 minutes. A similar flow also brings up a low-cost SO-ARM101 arm and an industrial grade FR3 Duo from scratch to live waving.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Once the robot is live, you can use a simple prompt to create a basic task within just a few minutes:

Once the robot is live, the next step is building a simulation you can trust. With the Robotics plugin, MuJoCo runs directly inside Antigravity with dedicated creation, auditing, and rendering skills. This allows you to build and preview high-quality simulated scenes and assets—whether described, imported from links/documents, or uploaded via workspace photos—right alongside your code. You can then "vibe sim" by adjusting shapes, positions, and physical properties through chat to verify reaching and grasping before deploying to physical hardware.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The workbench with Hydrogen and Robotics plugins lets you go from an idea to a repeatable working application in real world in a much shorter time, by iterating and validating it in simulation.

A developer starts with a whiteboard sketch: a conveyor belt carrying mixed snacks, with a robot arm picking and sorting them into bins. From one prompt of the task with images of a real scene, Hydrogen constructs an interactive MuJoCo simulation of the cell, testing and validating the grasping policy in sim across multiple runs. The developer then deploys the verified policy directly to the robot in the real world. When nuances emerge (such as tuning gripper closure force or refining sorting rules), the developer iterates in natural language, asking Hydrogen to adjust the controller to iterate. Within about an hour end-to-end, the arm reliably sorts snacks off the moving belt.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Hydrogen's efficiency makes it practical to run a multi-step robot application all day. In this example, a developer uses Hydrogen to build a small web storefront for a soap shop, then connects it to two YAM Pro arms. When an order comes in, the arms set out a packing bin, pick the ordered mint and citrus bars, and pull a printed thank-you note from a printer.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

With just a few prompts, the FR3 Duo simulates a chess board and then can play chess against itself or take on human opponents.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Gemini 4 Argon pushes the ceiling of physical reasoning. Gemini 4 Hydrogen makes those capabilities fast and affordable enough to use every day, and the Antigravity Robotics plugin puts them in a workbench where anyone can go from a robot in a box to a working application. We can't wait to see what you build.

You can share your own creations and use cases on the plugin’s GitHub discussions page. If you have any ideas, custom skills, or derived work you think would be helpful, please let us know—we’ll keep improving the plugins.

#10158. Hello Weather is the best Apple Weather app on Android

While Pixel Weather serves my needs quite well, I’ve been looking for a way to check Apple Weather (previously Dark Sky) forecast data on Android, and I’ve finally found it with Hello Weather by Electric Dream Machine.

Hello Weather started out on iOS before coming to Android a few years ago. On Apple devices, the app got a big redesign last year, and it just came to Android last month with a promise that updates will roll out to both platforms at the same time going forward.

The app has a four-tab layout with the bottom bar aligning with the latest Material 3 Expressive height, avoiding a common pitfall. One small customization is being able to turn on/off tab names.

Locations shows a list of all your saved cities, while Forecast is the main view. The current condition with a large icon — five different sets are available — appears first. Be sure to enable the stats row so you can see cards like Wind, Cloud Cover, and UV Index without having to tap for the bottom sheet. When open, you get the following stats presented as a grid: Today (temperature range), Precipitation, Wind, Humidity, UV Index, Cloud Cover, Visibility, Pressure, Air Quality, Pollen, Daylight, and Moon Phase.

The Hourly Forecast section is a scrollable carousel, with the Bonus Info toggle in Settings adding another stat. “Automatic” will intelligently select “relevant info for you.” You can tap to browse the hourly breakdown in a sheet view.

Finally, there’s the Daily Forecast section that gives you an eight-day overview via a temperature-range layout, which is my favorite way to visualize this information. Again, this can be tapped to get a bottom sheet explorer.

I've lost count on how many weather apps on Android I've tried out over the years but since the Pixel Weather app became a thing I've tried far fewer and the few I have tried while many have been prettier to look at, I've always ended up coming back to the stock PW app. It never hurts to have options though and this one looks like a good one. Thanks for the mention, I hadn't heard of it before.

The Radar tab is up next, with Google Maps powering this view. From the three-dot overflow menu, you can customize the Animation Speed, Map Layers, Style, and Options.

Settings is where you can make all the aforementioned customizations, including changing which Forecast Data source you’re using. The usual players are here, like The Weather Channel and AccuWeather. I also like the stylized in-app changelog.

In Notifications, you can have a persistent silent alert appear. There’s also the ability to receive Morning and Evening Reports, along with Precipitation and Severe Alerts. This is joined by a trio of homescreen widgets.
