#10154. ‘Old’ Galaxy phones are a better deal; here’s what you should buy

Old is a label, and saying last year’s Galaxy flagships from Samsung are dated or worthless is disingenuous. You can get a killer deal on a phone a generation or two old.

Beyond that, it still has a flagship SoC in the Snapdragon 8 Elite – the same 3nm foundation as the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5. It also takes on much of the same design, so if you like the rounded corners of the Galaxy S26 Ultra, you’ll be missing out on almost nothing by choosing the Galaxy S25 Ultra for a better deal.

Right now, a 1TB model Galaxy S25 Ultra can be had for $780 on Amazon. It’s a “renewed” model, but that’s an excellent choice over a used device, since Samsung isn’t making them anymore. That’s down from the original $1,659 price tag when it launched. It’s also a good bit cheaper than the best deal you can get on an S26 Ultra – $1,049 for 256GB model from Samsung.

You can get a slightly better deal on something like the Galaxy S24 Ultra, though it might not have the same upsides as the S25 Ultra. There are a couple of reasons for that.

First, it’s already in its second year of a seven-year update policy. In effect, you’re only getting five years of updates. Second, it doesn’t have the newest design language, if that’s something important to you.

On the flip side, you might prefer the boxier look Samsung used to run by on its devices.

The Galaxy S24 Ultra deal on Amazon that sits it at $591 right now also applies to the 512GB model, unlike the deal for a 1TB variant above. Still, it’s a solid deal for a phone that really isn’t that old, and it’s close to what the mid-range Galaxy A57 sells for, new.

It’ll still handle everyday tasks with next to no issues, and that makes it a great gift – possibly overkill for a first phone, to be frank.

The Galaxy S25 Plus is closer to a “first phone” than an Ultra model would be. It has excellent specs, but it only misses out on some of the raw power Samsung’s flagships have and an S Pen.

During Black Friday deal week on Amazon, it looks like an unlocked Galaxy S25 Plus goes for $683. It’s higher than the previous year’s Ultra, but it isn’t as expensive as the renewed Galaxy S25 Ultra. It has the same design language as Samsung’s new phones, and it still has six of the seven years left in its update lifetime.

It certainly beats paying over $900 for the new one, even with a deal.

This is also a good opportunity to get a foldable, if you’ve been eyeing the Galaxy Z Fold lineup. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 series is fantastic, and it blows other devices out of the water. But it’s pricey.

Instead of paying $1,499 for a Galaxy Z Fold 8 right now, even though that’s a great deal, you can get a 512GB Galaxy Z Fold 7 for $867 on Amazon. It’ll be sold under Amazon’s “Renewed” program, so it’ll be covered under some type of warranty.

Doing that is also a great way to get set up for a trade-in deal when the next set of Galaxy Z Fold 9 devices roll out.

Fun fact: Samsung is selling a refurbished Galaxy Z Fold 7 for the exact same price as the Galaxy Z Fold 8 while the new one is on sale. Obviously, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the better choice on Samsung’s website.

If you’re absolutely set on getting a Galaxy phone that came out in the last year, Samsung’s deals are better than anywhere else. When Samsung runs deals like it has right now, that’s the best time to buy a device. In any other case, it can be a hard bargain.

The Galaxy S26 Ultra is always a good choice, but it’s going to be $999 without a trade-in. That said, trade-in values are boosted right now. You can get $600 for something like your Galaxy S24 Ultra, but you can’t stack discounts. Trading a Galaxy S24 Ultra in brings the Galaxy S26 Ultra down to $799. Still a good deal.

Samsung’s devices offer the latest Android updates and features, even if they’re a couple of years old. Some of the Galaxy phones we knew as the “best” last year are affordable options right now.

#10152. 9to5Google’s Best of 2026

We reviewed dozens of products in 2026, and the one through line across all of them was cost. Because of RAMageddon, everything was more expensive, and it became somewhat easier to separate the products that were good because they were cheap from the ones that were good at any price.

This year was a lot of things, but it was primarily a reminder that the best products are created through careful iteration. Only one product on this list, the XREAL Aura, represents an emerging class. The rest, from the Fitbit Air to the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, showcase years of work in optimizing for design, weight, battery life, and performance. Some take more risks than others, but to us they all represent the very best of their respective categories.

Price: $99.99/$129.99 (special editions)Black Friday deal: Save $30 on the Google Fitbit Air Champagne Gold & Obsidian Bundle. Ends 12/02.Release date: May 26, 2026

A screenless Google device outside of a smart speaker wasn’t something I thought I needed. Yet the Fitbit Air has become one of my favorite products to come out of Mountain View in years. Fitness tracking is something I want happening quietly in the background, not another reason to check a screen.

That’s what the Air gets right. It isn’t trying to reinvent fitness tracking; it packages it in a comfortable, lightweight band that stays out of the way. Health tracking data isn’t locked behind a paywall, and there’s no display demanding attention throughout the day.

Smart rings and full-featured watches have their place, but this slim wearable strikes a particularly appealing balance. It gives me useful information when I want it without becoming another device to manage. It’s comfortable, inexpensive, and easy to live with—and sometimes that’s exactly what makes a product great.

— Damien Wilde, Daniel Bader & Andrew Romero

Price: $2,499Black Friday price: $1,899Release date: November 1, 2026

I’m primarily a TV person, especially when it comes to gaming. But after spending time with XGIMI’s Horizon 30 Pro, I’ve been enjoying a very different setup. I loved the older Horizon Pro 4K and used it until this arrived; the 30 Pro feels like a substantial leap forward.

VRR support has made it possible to play my Steam Machine on a roughly 200-inch image in the bedroom, while the built-in speakers do an impressive job of filling the room. Going from Forza Horizon 5 at 120fps to Blue Planet on BBC iPlayer is effortless thanks to Google TV.

The swiveling base is more useful than it might sound. I can position the projector in the corner of the room and let XGIMI’s keystone correction handle the rest. It’s an expensive upgrade, but for a home cinema setup that doubles as a giant gaming display, it’s a beast.

I still wish the Steam Controller cost less. Beyond that, though, I have very little to complain about: this is the best controller I’ve used in years. It’s comfortable, gives Windows users a way to control their PC, and brings together some of the best ideas from my beloved Steam Deck.

Steam Input is the real selling point. The depth of customization makes this controller feel at home on a PC in a way few alternatives do. I haven’t connected with a new controller this quickly in a long time.

That doesn’t mean everyone needs one. Most people would be well served by an 8BitDo controller—or even a Stadia controller converted to Bluetooth. But if you play on PC and want the premium option, this is my pick.

Price: $1,899.99Black Friday price: $1,549.99Release date: August 7, 2026

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is my favorite foldable of 2026. I’ve been enthralled with its design since reviewing it in July, and it won’t be leaving my daily rotation anytime soon.

The short, compact outer screen is refreshing to use, but it’s the inner display that changed the way I think about foldables. Its proportions feel more flexible than the square-shaped displays that have become so common across the category. Samsung’s motivations for adopting this form factor may be obvious, but the result works—and that’s what matters when I’m using it every day.

— Ben Schoon, Daniel Bader & Andrew Romero

Price: Ear (3): $179 / Ear (3a): $99Black Friday price: Ear (3): $94 / Ear (3a): $69Release date: Ear (3): Sept 18, 2025 / Ear (3a): July 7, 2026

Ear (3a) earns the nod here, but this is really a celebration of Nothing’s entire audio lineup. Its clean sound profiles, thoughtful designs, and compatibility with Android and the other devices I use have kept me listening all year.

Ear (3) remains my primary pair of earbuds, while Ear (3a) gets plenty of use when pocket space is at a premium. Ear (open) and Nothing Headphone (1) Pro round out the rest of my listening needs.

I still stand by calling Ear (3a) simply ‘the best earbuds.’ More broadly, Nothing has built an audio lineup worth paying attention to, especially when end-of-year discounts make it easier to try.

Price: ~$1,999Release date: April 21, 2026 (China) / Nov 2026 (Global)

If there’s one phone from 2026 I wish I could return to, it’s the Oppo Find X9 Ultra. ColorOS has quietly become one of the best Android experiences by getting the small details right, and the Ultra’s camera hardware and shooting experience remain standouts—even next to Oppo’s newer Find X10 Pro Max.

This is one of those increasingly rare smartphones that feels unwilling to compromise. I’ll be thinking about it until there’s a proper successor, and the prospect of not getting one in 2027 is disappointing.

I wish Oppo had sold this phone officially in the US. Importing comes with real complications, but the Find X9 Ultra is one of the few phones good enough to make me consider it.

Price: ~$1,720Release date: March 13, 2026

It’s been a remarkable year for foldables, but the Honor Magic V6 is my favorite of the bunch. Its combination of durability, battery life, and camera quality finally made me stop thinking about going back to a conventional slab phone.

The cameras are a big part of that. I used to rely entirely on my Panasonic GH5 for work photos; now I often reach for the Magic V6 instead. Its large 1/2-inch telephoto sensor helps it deliver the kind of dependable results I want when I need to capture something quickly.

It’s also held up to far more punishment than I’d normally put a foldable through. After a mud torture test and a trip through the dishwasher, I’m still using it months later. Battery life has been another major strength.

Honor’s software still has rough edges, but the improvements are substantial, and the seven-year update promise matters. The remaining problem is availability: I’d love to see Honor make its phones officially accessible across North America.

Android XR has had an awkward start, but the XREAL Aura feels like the kind of product the platform needed from the beginning. It puts a fresh idea into a form factor that’s easier to imagine wearing than a bulky headset.

Think of it as smart glasses that behave more like a VR headset. Open the app drawer with a pinch—or use the tethered compute puck—and your chosen app appears as a floating window you can move around. It’s like having a tablet suspended wherever you need it.

That makes remote work and entertainment feel surprisingly natural, especially when the glasses weigh less than a fifth of many VR headsets. There are compromises: hand gestures can be finicky, and while Bluetooth game controllers work, there isn’t a Meta Quest-style motion controller available for immersive gaming.

Even so, Aura makes a compelling case for Android XR. It’s an appealing option for people interested in immersive entertainment who don’t want the weight and bulk of a conventional VR headset.

DroidDeck has changed the way I think about gaming on Android. It isn’t the first project to bring PC games to a phone, but it feels like one of the most capable. The pitch is straightforward, even if the technology isn’t: run Steam on your Android device.

Rather than emulating an entire PC in the traditional sense, DroidDeck brings together translation layers using projects such as FEX and Proton. The technical details are complicated, but the practical result is that your phone can run games it wasn’t originally designed to understand.

Performance across my devices has been impressive—good enough that I’ve seen games run better than on my Steam Deck. The project is also free, community-driven, and highly customizable. The trade-off is that support for brand-new hardware can take time.

If you’ve been on the fence about mobile gaming, pairing DroidDeck with a controller such as my favorite, the GameSir G8 Galileo, is a great way to see how far it’s come.

Price: $179.99 / $219.99 (special editions)Release date: October 27, 2026

The Fitbit Air is a good fitness tracker and, to my eye, an attractive bracelet. But years of wearing watches have trained me to expect the time on my wrist. Even toward the end of my time with the Air, I kept glancing down for a clock that wasn’t there.

The Fitbit Edge fixes that central complaint. Its always-on display reduces battery life to roughly two or three days, but that’s a trade-off I’m happy to make. The software’s Pixel Watch-inspired look also helps it feel like part of a more cohesive lineup. For me, a small screen makes a meaningful difference.

Price: $29.99Release date: November 11, 2026

We waited so long for a first-party Google tracker that the Find My Device network had time to become Find Hub. The Pixel Tag makes the wait worthwhile with a more integrated experience: everything is managed in the Find Hub app, rather than spread across separate software.

‘Notify when left behind’ was overdue, and ‘Ring this phone’ has worked well for me. These are practical features, but they’re also reminders of how much first-party hardware can do to improve the software around it.

I also like the pill-shaped design. Google didn’t simply copy the AirTag; it made something that feels at home in the Pixel family. It’s a small thing, but it’s pleasant to hold—and surprisingly satisfying to fidget with.

Price: $349.99Black Friday price: $314.99Release date: July 6, 2026

A dock needs to do more than look good, but Keychron’s Thunderbolt 5 dock makes a strong first impression on a desk. Its machined aluminum case fits neatly into my setup, and the usefulness goes well beyond the finish.

I switched between devices more often this year. Moving my work from a Windows PC to the Mac mini M6 left the PC on gaming duty, while the Googlebook launch gave me another computer to plug into the same monitors and workstation.

The Keychron dock makes those changes simple: I connect the USB-C input cable and let it handle the display and data connections. Its own power supply, four USB-A ports, three USB-C ports, SD card reader, and wired Ethernet connection give me the flexibility I need. It might be the most useful addition I’ve made to my desk all year.

I don’t think I’ll stop talking about the Boox Picco anytime soon. It’s a wonderfully simple little e-reader with a straightforward operating system and battery life that stretches across days or weeks, depending on use.

You can attach it magnetically to the back of your phone or slip it into almost any pocket. But the bigger benefit is how easy it makes picking up a book, whether I’m out of the house or just moving from one room to another.

At around $100, it’s an appealingly focused device. In a year when so much attention has gone to retro gadgets and handhelds, the Picco captures a similar pleasure: a small piece of technology that does one thing well.

Price: $499.99Black Friday price: $399.99Release date: October 14, 2026

If one gadget came out of nowhere to steal the show for me in 2026, it was the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro. Amazon rebooted its tablet lineup, dropped the Fire branding, and embraced a much more familiar version of Android. Install a third-party launcher to remove the permanent Alexa search bar, and it feels like a regular mid-range Android tablet—with full Play Store support, no sideloading required.

The hardware is where it really wins me over. The base configuration pairs 8GB of RAM with 128GB of storage, which is more than enough for browsing, movies, YouTube, and casual games. But the display is the standout: this is a much better LCD than I expect from a tablet under $1,000, and the nanomatte finish looks and feels fantastic.

I’m as surprised as anyone that Amazon finally figured out its tablet lineup. A little help from the person behind Microsoft’s Surface line seems to have gone a long way. At this price—and especially on sale—I struggle to recommend anything else, including Apple’s base iPad. It’s that good.

Price: $99Black Friday price: $84Release date: July 21, 2026

I have always been a reader, but in my effort to spend less time on my phone this year, I did my utmost to return to e-paper displays for most long-form reading.

The XTEINK X4 Pro is a tiny 4-inch touch-capable e-paper display with a backlight and plenty of battery life. Its magnets help the device stick to the back of most phones (though it’s a bit tall for some smaller devices like the Pixel 11 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro), and it supports custom firmware through the Crosspoint project.

While it occasionally feels cramped to read on, the sheer number of formatting and font options makes it easy to adjust the reading experience to suit your tastes. And because it supports KOReader, reading progress syncs with most other devices, including Kobo e-readers and supported phone apps.

#10150. Big Google Home update adds these new Nest Cam, app, and Gemini features

#10149. Why the Agent Isn’t the Hard Part: Where Developers Go Wrong With Agentic Workflows

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can build an AI agent in an afternoon now. Pick a model, write a loop, hand it a few tools and watch it book a meeting or open a pull request. The demo works, and everyone in the room is impressed.

Then it meets a real system with real permissions and real customers, and things get quiet. Gartner has predicted that over 40% of agentic AI projects will be canceled by the end of 2027, citing rising costs, unclear business value and inadequate risk controls. Its analyst Anushree Verma described most current projects as early-stage experiments that are driven by hype and often misapplied. A forecast is not a measurement, but the pattern it describes matches what many teams report: the agent is rarely what breaks. The workflow around it is.

This article covers where that workflow tends to fail, with a focus on permissions, tool connections and human approval. It includes a small, runnable policy gate in JavaScript, compares the main safeguards and notes their limits. Standards and guidance change quickly in this area, so check the linked sources for the current versions.

A year or two ago the model was often the differentiator. Today many teams can reach the same frontier models through the same kinds of APIs, so if the intelligence is similar, the advantage has to come from somewhere else.

Anthropic’s guide to building effective agents reached a similar conclusion after working with teams building agents. It reports that the most successful implementations were not using complex frameworks or specialized libraries, and were building with simple, composable patterns instead. It also separates workflows, where code paths are predefined, from agents, where the model chooses its own steps, and advises using the simplest approach that works and adding complexity only when measurement shows it helps.

That should change where developers spend their time. Frameworks matter, but three less glamorous questions matter more: what can the agent touch, when does it stop, and who checks its work?

The OWASP guidance on Excessive Agency names what goes wrong here. It covers agents that can do more than they need to, and it traces the problem to three root causes: excessive functionality, excessive permissions and excessive autonomy.

In practice this looks familiar. An agent that only needs to read tickets is given a token that can also close them. A summarization tool connects with an admin account because that was quicker to set up. A generic “run shell command” tool is exposed when the agent only ever needs to write one file.

The fix is ordinary engineering discipline applied to a new kind of caller. OWASP’s recommendations include giving each tool only the functions and permissions it needs, preferring narrow tools over open-ended ones, running actions under the user’s own scoped identity, requiring human approval for high-impact actions and enforcing authorization in the downstream system rather than letting the model decide. The last point is the important one: a model can be talked out of a rule written in a prompt, but an API that checks scopes cannot.

Make the risky properties of a tool explicit in its definition so your orchestration layer can act on them:

Nothing clever is happening there, which is the point. The decision about what needs a human is made by a developer at design time, not by a model at runtime.

The definitions above only help if something enforces them. The sketch below is a policy gate that sits between the model and your tools. It checks that the tool exists, that the caller was granted the scope, and whether the action is reversible, and it records each decision. It runs on Node.js 18 or later with no dependencies.

A short test file shows the behavior. Unknown tools and missing scopes are denied, reads run, and the irreversible send waits for a person:

This is deliberately minimal. A real system would load scopes from the user’s actual session, enforce the same scopes again in the downstream API, and store the audit log somewhere durable. The gate is a second line of defense, not a replacement for authorization in the systems the agent calls.

The Model Context Protocol has become a common way to connect agents to tools. Anthropic donated MCP to the Agentic AI Foundation, a directed fund under the Linux Foundation, in December 2025. Block contributed goose and OpenAI contributed AGENTS.md as founding projects, which shows how central the ecosystem has become.

A standard protocol is good news. It also means you can install a tool server in minutes and expose every endpoint of your issue tracker, repository or CRM to your agent, whether it needs them or not. Easy connection quietly pushes more power to the agent.

The protocol’s own security best practices document covers several relevant risks, including confused deputy problems in proxy servers, token passthrough, local server compromise and scope minimization. For scopes, it recommends starting with a minimal set and elevating only when a privileged operation is first attempted, and it lists wildcard or omnibus scopes as a common mistake.

A sensible rule is to treat every tool server like a third-party dependency running with production credentials. Review the tools it exposes, switch off the ones the workflow does not use, pin versions and log every call. If you would not let a new contractor do it on day one, the agent should not do it either.

Human-in-the-loop design is often implemented as “approve everything”. That fails in a predictable way: people click approve forty times a day and stop reading by lunchtime.

A better rule is to gate on reversibility. Reads run freely, reversible writes run and get logged, and irreversible actions such as sending, paying or deleting wait for a person. Everything leaves a trace that a developer can replay when something looks odd. The gate above implements exactly this split.

Some teams build these pieces themselves, and others buy them. Elsewhen, a London-based consultancy that works on agentic operations for enterprise teams, describes its own service in similar terms. Its page lists approval gates where people sign off consequential decisions, an audit trail of what each agent read and which systems it touched, and a shared layer of permissions and guardrails that is reused across workflows. Elsewhen also says its Planet Agent report found that agents tend to start at the edges of an organization, where tasks are repeatable and mistakes are cheap, and then move toward the core. Treat that as the vendor’s own finding, and ask for the underlying evidence before relying on it.

The same logic applies outside engineering. The first question worth asking any AI partner is what they have actually put into production, and developers can ask the same of their own work.

No single control is enough. The table summarizes what each safeguard does well and where it falls short.

It is the OWASP term for an agent that can do more than it needs to, caused by excessive functionality, permissions or autonomy.

A prompt rule can be bypassed or ignored, while a scope check in the downstream API is enforced regardless of what the model says.

Usually not. Gating on reversibility keeps humans focused on actions such as sending, paying and deleting, and avoids approval fatigue.

Treat them like any third-party dependency. Review the tools they expose, limit scopes, pin versions and log calls.

The agentic projects that survive the next two years will probably not be the ones with the most ambitious autonomy. They will be the ones where someone sat down early and decided what the agent could touch, what it had to ask about and how anyone would know what it did.

That work does not demo well and it will not trend on social media. It is still the part that decides whether your agent becomes infrastructure or ends up among the projects that get cancelled.

#10147. Motorola Signature 27 hands-on: Pure elegance on display

It’s hard to believe we finally got a proper premium phone from Motorola again, and it feels a bit like the return of the Moto X.

Motorola’s slow regeneration in North America from a mostly budget device-minded company to one offering the most expensive non-folding flagship phone might become the thing of legend. That’s not to say the company hasn’t shown true greatness in the past — the Moto X, Moto Z, and Razr lines are proof of that — but it’s always shied away from pulling out all the stops until the Motorola Signature 27 arrived.

Freshly unboxed and sitting in my hands, the textile-clad Capulet Olive Motorola Signature 27 colorway has impressed me in every way, shape, and form over the past 24 hours. It’s a much longer period of time than I usually get with hands-on opportunities and, yet, the newness of the experience has yet to wear off.

Part and parcel to that experience is the design. For whatever reason, this is the year Motorola seems to have truly figured out what makes a phone nice to hold. The textile back, ribbed metal frame, rounded corners, and curved edges on the front and back of the phone are purely blissful to hold. It’s a reminder that a phone can be as good looking as it feels instead of being a flat, boring, off-balance slab like too many phones are now.

The weight also surprised me. On paper this sits right in the middle of all the Ultra/Pro Max-named phones, yet it feels lighter for some intangible reason. The camera hump makes it slightly top-heavy, and it’s almost as wobble-free as a Pixel (there’s just one off-center camera hump).

The new 120Hz LTPO tandem OLED display is as top-notch as you’re going to get. I had all of maybe 2 hours of direct sunlight yesterday to test it with, and boy does it ever get bright. The rest of the time, I’ve been either inside or out in overcast weather, all of which it looked brilliant in. And it uses a combination of DC dimming above 20% brightness, and 2160Hz PWM dimming below 20% brightness, making it super comfortable for me.

Motorola’s software has evolved again with this release, and the verdict is still out on whether or not this is actually better than the company’s Android 16 designs. The build of Android 17 that ships with this phone utilizes some major design changes we’ve only seen on a few random low-end Motorola phones, and I’m not sure I care for the gentle gradients on the quick toggle icons and some other system UI elements. It feels like a weirdly cheap effect that clashes with Material Design’s flatter motif.

Despite that, this is the most Pixelly (is that a word?) a Motorola phone has ever felt. Features like Rambler, Sign to Text, and Create My Widget are here and work impeccably well. Gemini Intelligence is also on the way, and while there’s no specific ETA for that at the moment, it’s exciting to know that the most advanced Android features — which are currently only available on a very small number of devices — are coming to this phone.

This may also be the fastest phone I’ve used to date. Everything is unbelievably snappy. Tap an icon and the thing opens instantly. Animations are zippy, and the new lock screen and customization options feel cohesive. Plus, all the favorite Moto actions and gestures are still here, which I greatly appreciate.

I’ve got some lofty expectations for this camera hardware, too, and it’s because Motorola is talking up such a big game. DXOMark’s camera review rated it the 2nd-best smartphone camera in the world, just under the Huawei Pura 80 Ultra and above the iPhone 18 Pro. Some people don’t take much stock in DXOMark, but it’s still worth noting that they’ve praised the heck out of the camera and Motorola is also pushing this as a winner in a way they’ve never done before.

The hype for the Motorola Signature 27 certainly seems to be realized, and now only time will truly tell if it can live up to the lofty expectations Motorola’s $1,599 price has set for it. If nothing, it’s certainly off to a good start and has made an excellent first impression.

#10137. The Motorola Signature 27’s haptics finally feel more like a Pixel, and it’s about time

Motorola has been content with mid-grade haptics for years, but the Motorola Signature 27’s haptics rightfully upgrade the experience.

Two days ago, I unboxed the Motorola Signature 27 and talked about my experience a bit in my Motorola Signature 27 hands-on. It’s as gorgeous as the pictures make it look, and the texture on the back of my “Capulet Olive” green colorway is the stuff of dreams. But the thing that sealed the new ultra-high-quality design for me wasn’t the comfortable curved edges or the textile back: it was the haptic motors.

Now, don’t get me wrong. The Motorola Signature 27’s build and design are probably my favorite of the year. I’ll need to use it for just a bit longer to be 100% sure, but the pebble-like aesthetic of the curved front, back, and edges feels tangibly amazing to hold and use. It reminds me a bit of the Galaxy S3’s design, just without the nasty, slimy plastic back. It’s curved just enough to feel amazing, but not enough to be an annoyance like some waterfall display phones were in the past.

But a haptic motor is completely intangible to you as the reader. You can’t see it in pictures, and even if I describe it, you may not fully understand why it makes such a difference. If you’ve been using Google Pixel phones for a long time, you’ve probably forgotten how good a quality haptic motor can feel, but let me remind you for a moment just how important such an invisible thing can be to completing the experience.

Typing on the Signature 27 is pure bliss because of it. Tapping each key feels more like the press of a physical keyboard key. Unlocking the phone emits a satisfying light punch to my thumb, signalling a successful read. When I send a reply on X (Twitter), the button tap and subsequent animation feel like the message is being sent through the phone onto the web. It’s wonderful, and it’s one of the reasons people love the Pixel experience so much.

Unfortunately, the Motorola Signature 27’s haptic responses aren’t applied to as many parts of the UI as Pixel phones do, and that’s where the real miss comes in. Adjusting the brightness or volume doesn’t feel like a physically rotating vertical dial the way it does on Pixel, and I’d love to see Motorola add this in an update. But at least the upgraded haptic engine is a huge step in the right direction for Motorola and a good way to make the Signature 27 feel like a proper flagship phone.

#10136. Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

We’d like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Haamed Gheibi, Erik Sanders, Rob Seymour, Taylor Santiago, Andrew Hsieh, Eric Taseski, Kiyoung Kim, Hung-Wei Chen, Jose “Pepe” Galmes, Ronald Li, Yu-Che Wang, Roger Ellis, Sandeep Patil, Amith Dsouza and the entire Android Build team

#10128. A Practical Guide to Browser Developer Tools for Debugging and Improving Websites

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a website involves more than writing HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. Developers also need to test how their code behaves in different browsers, identify layout problems, investigate JavaScript errors, and understand why certain pages take longer to load.

Browser developer tools make these tasks easier. They provide a way to inspect the code currently running in a browser without constantly changing files or guessing what might be wrong.

Whether you're developing a new website or maintaining an existing application, learning to use these tools can improve your debugging workflow and help you write more reliable code.

Let's explore some useful browser development tools and how to apply them to everyday web development tasks.

Browser developer tools, commonly called DevTools, are built-in utilities available in modern browsers such as Google Chrome, Mozilla Firefox, Microsoft Edge, and Safari.

They allow developers to examine a webpage's HTML structure, modify CSS properties, debug JavaScript, inspect network requests, and test responsive layouts.

You can usually open them by right-clicking a webpage and selecting Inspect. On Windows and Linux, the F12 key or Ctrl + Shift + I shortcut also opens DevTools in most browsers.

The following table highlights some commonly used features:

Understanding what each panel does makes it easier to select the right tool for a particular problem.

The Elements panel is one of the most frequently used features in browser DevTools.

It displays the Document Object Model (DOM), which represents the structure of the webpage currently loaded in the browser. Developers can select individual elements and examine the CSS rules affecting their appearance.

For example, imagine that a heading has more spacing than expected. Instead of immediately editing the stylesheet, inspect the heading and review its computed styles.

You might discover that the spacing comes from a margin inherited through another CSS rule or from a conflicting selector.

You can also experiment with CSS properties directly inside DevTools.

If the card appears too wide, you can temporarily change its width or padding in the Styles panel.

The browser immediately displays the result, allowing you to compare different values without repeatedly editing and saving your stylesheet.

However, these temporary changes do not automatically update your original CSS files. Once you've identified the correct solution, apply it to your project source code.

For more detailed CSS debugging techniques, developers can also read SitePoint's guide on CSS Debugging and Optimization: Browser-based Developer Tools.

JavaScript errors can prevent interactive features from working correctly. A navigation menu might stop opening, a form might fail to submit, or a button might not trigger the expected action.

The Console panel helps developers investigate these problems.

For example, consider the following JavaScript:

If the page does not contain an element with the ID submitButton, the selected value will be null. Calling addEventListener() on that value causes an error.

You can prevent this particular issue by checking whether the element exists:

The Console panel also allows developers to execute JavaScript expressions manually.

This returns the number of image elements currently present in the document.

You can use similar expressions to inspect elements, check values, and test small pieces of functionality.

Sometimes a JavaScript error is not immediately visible. A function may execute successfully but produce an unexpected result.

In such cases, the Sources panel in Chrome DevTools or the Debugger panel in Firefox can help.

A breakpoint pauses JavaScript execution at a specific line. Developers can then inspect variable values, examine the call stack, and execute the code step by step.

If the application displays an incorrect total, you can place a breakpoint inside calculateTotal().

When the function executes, the browser pauses and allows you to inspect the values of price, quantity, and total.

This is more useful than repeatedly adding console.log() statements throughout a large application.

Breakpoints are particularly helpful when debugging complex functions, asynchronous operations, and event handlers.

A webpage may contain several resources, including images, stylesheets, JavaScript files, fonts, and API requests.

If one of these resources fails to load, part of the website may stop working correctly.

The Network panel provides information about requests made by the browser.

It displays request URLs, HTTP status codes, response sizes, loading times, and other useful details.

For example, the following table explains some common HTTP responses developers may encounter:

Suppose an image is missing from a product page. Open the Network panel, reload the page, and filter the requests by images.

If the image returns a 404 response, check whether the file path is correct and whether the resource exists on the server.

The same process can help investigate failed API requests.

For example, if a frontend application requests product information from an API, the Network panel allows you to inspect the request method, response status, and returned data.

This helps developers distinguish between frontend problems and backend errors.

A website that looks correct on a desktop computer may not work properly on a smartphone.

Text can overflow, navigation menus can become difficult to use, and images may extend beyond their containers.

Browser DevTools include responsive design and device emulation features that allow developers to test different viewport sizes.

In Chrome, you can enable the device toolbar from the DevTools interface. Firefox provides a similar feature called Responsive Design Mode.

The desktop layout displays three columns, while smaller screens display one column.

You can test this behavior by changing the viewport width inside DevTools.

A useful responsive testing process includes:

Checking common mobile and tablet widths.

Testing both portrait and landscape orientations.

Checking whether navigation remains usable.

Verifying that buttons and links are easy to interact with.

Device emulation is useful during development, but it does not completely replace testing on physical devices.

A website can have correct HTML, CSS, and JavaScript but still provide a poor experience if it loads slowly or performs unnecessary work.

The Performance panel helps developers investigate browser activity over time.

It can record page interactions and provide information about scripting, rendering, painting, and other browser operations.

For example, a page might become unresponsive when a user opens a navigation menu. A performance recording can help identify whether a long-running JavaScript function is blocking the main thread.

Developers can also use tools such as Lighthouse to audit performance, accessibility, SEO, and other aspects of a webpage.

Instead of making random optimization changes, use the available measurements to identify the actual problem.

Some useful areas to investigate include:

Performance analysis should be repeated after making changes to determine whether the optimization actually improves the result.

These features are useful, but they can also introduce debugging challenges.

For example, an application might continue displaying outdated information because an old value remains stored in local storage.

The Application panel in Chrome DevTools allows developers to inspect and manage several types of browser storage.

If the application behaves unexpectedly because of stored data, inspecting local storage can help identify the problem.

Be careful when clearing storage because doing so may remove authentication sessions, saved preferences, or other application data.

Knowing individual DevTools features is useful, but developers benefit even more from using them in a consistent order.

The following workflow can make troubleshooting more efficient:

This process helps developers identify the cause of a problem before changing multiple parts of the application.

It also makes debugging easier when working with other developers because the issue can be reproduced and explained using specific observations.

Browser developer tools are essential for modern web development. They help developers inspect HTML and CSS, debug JavaScript, analyze network requests, test responsive layouts, and investigate performance issues.

The most effective approach is to select the appropriate tool for the problem instead of making changes based on assumptions.

Whether you're working on a small website or a complex web application, becoming familiar with DevTools can make your development workflow more efficient and help you build websites that are easier to maintain.

Rather than treating debugging as something that happens only after development, make it a regular part of your workflow. This allows you to identify problems earlier, verify your solutions, and deliver more reliable web experiences.

#10124. How to Add Idempotency Keys to a Node.js API with PostgreSQL

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A mobile app sends POST /orders. The server creates the order, but the response is lost when the phone switches from Wi-Fi to 4G. The app sees a timeout and retries. Now the customer has two orders, and your support team has a refund to process.

Retrying is the right thing for a client to do. The problem is that POST is not idempotent: RFC 9110 defines GET, PUT and DELETE as idempotent methods, but not POST or PATCH. Repeating a POST can repeat its side effects.

An idempotency key fixes this. The client generates a unique key for each logical operation and sends it with every attempt. The server does the work once, stores the response against the key, and returns the stored response for any retry. The IETF HTTPAPI working group is standardizing this pattern as the Idempotency-Key HTTP header field, and this tutorial follows its recommendations.

We'll build it with Node.js, Express 5 and PostgreSQL. By the end you'll have a table that stores keys, a function that safely claims a key under concurrent requests, an Express route that replays stored responses, and a client that retries without creating duplicates.

The client's job is simple: generate a key, usually a UUID, and send the same key on every retry of the same operation. The draft defines the header value as a quoted string:

The server's job is harder, because every request falls into one of five cases:

To tell case 3 from case 4, the server stores a fingerprint of the request alongside the key. A matching fingerprint means a genuine retry; a different one means key reuse. Case 5 is the one most implementations get wrong, because it only shows up under concurrency. A database constraint handles it for us.

Create a project and install the two dependencies:

The key table records who sent the key, the request fingerprint, whether the work has finished, and the response to replay:

Two details matter here. The primary key includes client_id, so keys are scoped per client: two customers who happen to generate the same value can't see each other's responses. And locked_until lets the server recover a key whose request crashed halfway, which we'll use in Step 3.

The draft defines the header value as a structured field string, which means it arrives wrapped in double quotes. Many clients send it without quotes, so the parser below accepts both and limits the characters and length it will store:

The fingerprint has to be identical for identical requests, even if a client serializes the JSON body with its properties in a different order. Sorting object keys before hashing takes care of that. The hash uses SHA-256 from Node's built-in crypto module:

Including the method and path means a key reused on a different endpoint counts as a mismatch, not as a retry.

The tempting approach is to SELECT the key, and insert it if nothing comes back. Under concurrency that fails: two retries arriving a few milliseconds apart both see no row, and both do the work.

Instead, let the primary key decide. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING either inserts the row or does nothing, atomically, even across many application processes. The PostgreSQL INSERT documentation describes this behavior in detail. If the insert returns a row, this request owns the key. If not, we read the existing row and decide which case we're in:

The UPDATE in the last step is also atomic. If two retries find the same expired lock, only one of them matches the locked_until < now() condition and gets the key. If the row disappears between the two queries, for example because it was released after a failure, the function reports in_progress and the client simply retries.

Set LOCK_SECONDS comfortably above the slowest time your operation takes. If the lock expires while the original request is still running, a retry can take over and run the work a second time.

Once a request owns the key, it creates the order and marks the key as completed. Both writes happen in one transaction, so there is no moment where the order exists but the key still says in_progress, or the other way round. Note that a transaction in node-postgres has to use a single client checked out from the pool, as the node-postgres transaction documentation explains:

releaseKey runs when the work fails. Deleting the claim lets the client retry with the same key instead of waiting for the lock to expire. It only deletes rows that are still in progress, so a completed response is never thrown away.

The route validates the body first. A request with invalid input never touches the key table, so the client can fix the input and resend with the same key. Error responses use the application/problem+json format from RFC 9457, which the idempotency draft also uses in its examples:

The server side only works if the client keeps the key stable. The most common client bug is generating a new key inside the retry loop, which turns every retry into a new operation. Generate the key once per logical action, before the first attempt:

The catch branch is the reason idempotency keys exist. After a timeout, the client can't know whether the order was created. With a stable key, retrying is safe either way: if the first attempt succeeded, the retry returns the stored order; if it didn't, the retry creates it. The random jitter spreads retries out, so many clients recovering from the same outage don't all hit the server at once.

In a browser or mobile app, store the key with the pending action, for example in the form state or a local queue. If the user taps "Place order" again after an error, reuse it. Only generate a new key when the user starts a genuinely new action.

Keys can't live forever, or the table grows without limit. The draft leaves the retention period to the server but says it should be published in the API documentation, so clients know how long a retry stays safe. Twenty-four hours covers most retry scenarios. A scheduled job can delete older rows in batches:

Run it every few minutes from cron or your job scheduler until it deletes zero rows. Deleting in batches keeps each transaction short, and the created_at index from Step 1 keeps the inner query fast. A retry that arrives after its key has expired is treated as a new request, which is why the retention period belongs in your documentation.

Start the server with node server.js, then send the same request twice:

The second request lists the properties in a different order, and it's still recognized as a retry because of the canonical fingerprint. Both responses carry the same order ID, and the orders table contains one row.

Now change the quantity while keeping the key. The server answers 422, because the key now belongs to a different request:

To see the concurrent case, add await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 3000)); before completeKey in the route, restart the server, and fire two requests with a new key at the same time:

One request prints 201 and the other 409. Without the ON CONFLICT claim, both would have printed 201 and created two orders. Remove the delay when you're done.

This design guarantees that the database work in Step 4 happens once per key. Two situations need extra care:

Idempotency keys don't replace other safeguards either. A unique constraint on a natural business key, such as one payment per invoice, still catches duplicates that arrive with different keys, for example when a user submits the same form from two tabs.

With one table, an atomic claim and a transaction that stores the response next to the work, POST endpoints become safe to retry. Clients get a simple rule: generate one key per action, reuse it on every retry. The server takes care of the rest, including the concurrent retries that a SELECT-then-INSERT approach misses.

The same pattern works for PATCH endpoints and for webhooks you send to other systems. Start with the endpoints where a duplicate costs real money or confuses a customer, and publish your key format and retention period in the API documentation so client developers know what to send.

#10116. How SD Card Photo and Video Recovery Works: A Practical Guide With a JPEG Carving Example

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

For photographers, videographers and content creators, an SD card is more than a storage device. It holds the raw material behind a finished project: wedding photographs, travel footage, product demos and drone recordings that may be impossible to recreate. Data loss can happen at almost any stage of that workflow, from a folder deleted to make space to a card formatted before the footage was backed up.

This article explains why files on a card become unavailable, what to do in the first minutes, how recovery tools find data, and how to compare options. It includes a small Python example that recovers JPEG files from a disk image, so you can see the mechanism for yourself. It also compares free open-source tools with paid graphical ones, using criteria you can apply yourself. Recovery is never guaranteed, and tool features change, so confirm details against each vendor’s current documentation.

Recovery is not just a matter of finding a deleted filename. Several different problems can make files unavailable, and they call for different approaches.

These steps cannot guarantee recovery, but they reduce avoidable risk.

Most tools combine two methods, and understanding them helps you read scan results.

Carving has a cost. Because file system information is ignored, names and folder structure are usually lost, which is why scan results often show generic names. Fragmented files can also be recovered incomplete, since the tool assumes the pieces are stored one after another. Free tools build on these same ideas. Wondershare Recoverit V15, which also offers a free version for Windows and Mac, adds a graphical interface, while PhotoRec and ddrescue stay closer to the command line.

The script below is a teaching sketch, not a recovery product. It reads a raw disk image, finds JPEG start markers, and writes out the bytes up to the last end marker before the next start. That detail matters because a JPEG can contain an embedded thumbnail with its own markers, so stopping at the first end marker would cut a file short.

A short test builds two real JPEGs, hides them in random bytes, and checks that both are carved and open as valid images:

Use it on an image rather than the card itself. On Linux you can make one with a read-only copy, for example with the GNU ddrescue tool, which is designed to copy data from failing media. Then run the carver against the image and save the output to a different drive. Real tools handle many more formats, trim files using header information, and deal with video containers, so treat this as a way to understand the idea.

The right choice depends on the problem, your platform and how comfortable you are with the command line. The table summarizes what each project’s own documentation states.

The order of steps matters more than the choice of tool. A cautious workflow on Linux or macOS looks like this:

Graphical tools follow the same logic and mainly change step 3, with thumbnails, filters and search that make large result sets easier to browse. Whether that is worth paying for depends on how many files you need to sift through and how comfortable you are on the command line. When comparing any tool, ask:

Features such as search by capture time, folder or AI-based tagging depend on metadata surviving the loss, and they narrow results without proving a file is intact. Treat them as convenience, not verification.

Recovering a video file does not necessarily mean recovering a playable video. A recording interrupted during writing can lack the structural information a player needs, and other files may be damaged in ways that block playback.

The distinction matters. Recovery retrieves missing or inaccessible data, while repair tries to fix damage that affects a file’s usability. Some cases need one, the other or both. Repair has limits too: if essential video data was overwritten or never recorded, software cannot recreate the original footage in full. After any recovery or repair, play the file all the way through and compare it with what you expect.

For a quick check of a recovered clip, ffprobe can show whether the container is readable:

An error or a missing duration suggests the file needs repair or is incomplete.

Often yes, if nothing has overwritten them. Stop using the card immediately and scan it or an image of it.

Not before recovery. Formatting changes file system structures and may make recovery harder.

Recovery retrieves missing data. Repair fixes a file that exists but will not open or play correctly.

Not always. PhotoRec and TestDisk are free and open source, while paid tools add a graphical interface and extras such as previews, filtering and video repair.

On a different drive from the card. Saving to the same card can overwrite data you still want.

SD card data loss creates two related challenges: getting files back, and working out which recovered files are useful. Stopping use of the card, working on an image and understanding the difference between file system recovery, carving and repair put you in a better position, whichever tool you choose. Graphical tools aim to make large result sets easier to navigate, and free tools such as PhotoRec and TestDisk remain solid options. Try your chosen tool on a copy first, verify what you recover, and keep a backup so you do not need to do this again.

#10108. What an AI Marketing Agent Actually Does, and How to Build a Guarded One

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An AI marketing agent is a system that pairs a language model with access to real tools, such as a CRM, an ad platform API or a CMS, and lets it work toward a goal instead of following a fixed script. That is the core idea. Much of what gets labeled agentic marketing is either a wrapper around that loop or a scheduled job with a new name.

This article breaks down the mechanics in code terms, walks through a minimal guarded agent loop with tests, and gives a framework for deciding what to build, what to buy and what to keep away from any autonomous system. Product capabilities change quickly, so check each vendor's current documentation.

● An agent is a goal, a tool set and a loop. The model chooses the next action at runtime instead of following branches you wrote in advance.

● Guardrails belong in code. Spend caps, allow-lists and approval thresholds should never depend on the prompt.

● The prototype is the easy part. Validation, retries, logging and evaluation are where the weeks go.

● Match autonomy to reversibility. Let agents do reversible, measurable work and keep irreversible actions supervised.

● Treat vendor claims as claims. Ask for logs and test on your own data.

Many marketing features have been renamed in the last couple of years. Email sequencing, lead scoring and campaign tools are now often described as agents. The honest question is whether a given product is a real architecture shift or marketing automation with a chat box added. Both exist: some systems run a perceive-reason-act loop with dynamic tool selection, while others are if/else branches with a model writing the subject line.

Strip away the marketing language and an agent has three parts:

● A goal, expressed in natural language or as a measurable target.

● A tool set: functions the model is allowed to call, each described by a schema.

● A loop that runs until the goal is met, a limit is hit or a human steps in.

The reasoning step is a model call. You pass it the current state, the goal and the available tools, and it responds with text or a structured request to call a tool. Your code runs the call, feeds the result back and asks again. OpenAI's function calling guide describes this as a conversation between your application and the model, which is the primitive underneath most agent frameworks.

Compare that with a cron job. A cron job runs a rule such as pausing ads below a click-through threshold every morning. It cannot decide that cost per acquisition matters more than click-through this week. An agent can, because the branching is chosen at runtime by a model rather than at write time by you. A useful test: if you can draw the full decision tree before the system runs, it is automation. If the path depends on what the model decides after seeing the data, it is an agent.

● Perceive. Read current state from an ad platform's reporting endpoint, a CRM webhook or a database. Ordinary code, no model involved.

● Reason. One model call containing the goal, the state, a summary of past actions and the schemas of every tool the agent may use.

● Act. The model returns a structured tool call. Your code validates the arguments against the schema and your guardrails, then executes.

● Observe. Capture the result, including failures. A rate-limit error or validation error is information for the next step.

● Update memory. Record the action and outcome so the agent does not repeat itself.

● Repeat or stop. Loop until the goal is met, an iteration cap is reached or a guardrail escalates to a human.

Steps two and three rely on function calling, where tools are declared with typed parameters and the model returns matching arguments. The Hugging Face function calling guide shows the same pattern. For connecting tools across systems, the Model Context Protocol is an open protocol for integrating LLM applications with external data sources and tools. Instead of writing an adapter for every CRM and ad account, you point an MCP client at a server that exposes those tools with discoverable schemas.

Lead scoring and routing. The agent reads CRM state, enriches it with lookups, scores the lead and writes back an owner and next action. It is agentic when it chooses which enrichment tools to call based on what is missing, and not agentic when the score is a fixed model output piped into a rule.

Ad creative generation and testing. The agent produces variations from brand inputs, launches a structured test, reads performance data and shifts budget toward what works. This is among the clearest closed feedback loops because the outcome signal is numeric and fast.

IBM's overview of AI agents in marketing frames a similar progression, from scripted chatbots at one end to agents that decide, design workflows and use function calling at the other. Most products sit somewhere in between.

Vendors sometimes blur this line. Four criteria help even from the outside:

Practical tests: give it a goal it has no preset workflow for and see whether it composes a path or returns an apology. Change the data mid-run and check whether the next step differs. Ask for the tool call log, because a real agent produces one. Ask what happens when a tool call fails; a script throws, while an agent should retry or re-plan.

Three questions decide this, and none are about how clever the model is: can errors be detected quickly, can the action be reversed, and what does one bad decision cost?

● Reasonable to hand over: drafting copy variations, summarizing campaign performance, flagging data mismatches, writing internal briefs, pausing clearly underperforming ads.

● Keep supervised: budget reallocation across accounts, legal, medical or financial claims, pricing and discount changes, messages to named customers and final brand-voice sign-off.

A human approval step costs minutes. An unsupervised mistake in those areas can cost far more.

For many teams the practical answer is to build the orchestration that touches proprietary data and internal systems, and to evaluate off-the-shelf tools for narrow, high-volume tasks that other teams have already solved. What people underestimate when building is not the prototype, which can take an afternoon, but everything after: schema validation, retries, idempotency, rate-limit handling, prompt versioning, cost controls, an audit log and an evaluation harness.

Ad creative is a common candidate for the existing-tool route because volume is high and the loop is similar across businesses. Vendors in this space describe their products differently, so compare scope, integrations and pricing. As one example, Larven describes an AI marketing agent that runs Meta ads, pulls products and copy from a store such as Shopify, and connects to your own ad account. Treat any such description as the vendor's own, and verify it with a trial on your data before committing.

The goal: keep spend inside a daily cap while letting a model propose budget changes. The model is injected as a function, so you can wire in any provider's function-calling API or a stub for tests. The important part is that every limit is enforced in code. The model proposes, and your code decides.

"""A minimal guarded agent loop. The model is injected as a function, so you can swap in any provider's function-calling API, or a stub for tests.""" from dataclasses import dataclass, field @dataclass class Guardrails:     allowed_campaigns: set     daily_cap: float            # max total daily budget across campaigns     max_change_pct: float = 0.2 # larger single changes need a human     max_actions: int = 5        # per run @dataclass class State:     budgets: dict               # campaign_id -> daily budget     log: list = field(default_factory=list)     pending: list = field(default_factory=list) def check(call, state, g):     """Return 'run', 'approve' or ('deny', reason). Enforced in code, never by the prompt."""     if call["name"] != "adjust_budget":         return ("deny", "unknown tool")     cid, new = call["args"]["campaign_id"], call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]     if cid not in g.allowed_campaigns or cid not in state.budgets:         return ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     if new < 0:         return ("deny", "negative budget")     others = sum(v for k, v in state.budgets.items() if k != cid)     if others + new > g.daily_cap:         return ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     old = state.budgets[cid]     if old and abs(new - old) / old > g.max_change_pct:         return "approve"     return "run" def run(model, state, g):     """model(state) -> a tool call dict, or None when it has nothing to do."""     for in range(g.maxactions):         call = model(state)         if call is None:             break         verdict = check(call, state, g)         state.log.append({"call": call, "verdict": verdict})         if verdict == "run":             state.budgets[call["args"]["campaign_id"]] = call["args"]["new_daily_budget"]         elif verdict == "approve":             state.pending.append(call)     return state

The tests use a stub in place of a model. They confirm that small changes run, large changes wait for approval, the daily cap and allow-list are enforced, an unknown tool is denied, and a model that never stops is halted by the action limit with everything logged:

from agent_loop import Guardrails, State, check, run G = Guardrails(allowed_campaigns={"a", "b"}, daily_cap=200) def call(cid, new): return {"name": "adjust_budget", "args": {"campaign_id": cid, "new_daily_budget": new}} def test_small_change_runs():     assert check(call("a", 105), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "run" def test_large_change_needs_approval():     assert check(call("a", 150), State({"a": 100, "b": 50}), G) == "approve" def test_cap_and_allow_list_are_enforced():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 90, "c": 10})     assert check(call("a", 115), s, G) == ("deny", "daily cap exceeded")     assert check(call("c", 11), s, G) == ("deny", "campaign not allowed")     assert check({"name": "delete_account", "args": {}}, s, G) == ("deny", "unknown tool") def test_loop_stops_at_action_limit_and_logs_everything():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500, max_actions=3)     run(lambda st: call("a", st.budgets["a"] + 1), s, g)   # a model that never stops     assert len(s.log) == 3 and s.budgets["a"] == 103 def test_denied_and_pending_actions_do_not_change_budgets():     s = State({"a": 100, "b": 50})     g = Guardrails({"a", "b"}, 500)     seq = iter([call("a", 150), call("a", 600), None])   # needs approval, then breaks the cap     run(lambda st: next(seq), s, g)     assert s.budgets["a"] == 100 and len(s.pending) == 1

Run them with pytest. In a real deployment, the model function would call your provider with the state, the goal and the tool schemas, then return the parsed tool call.

● It handles one tool. A real agent needs several, such as reporting, pausing ads and requesting approval.

● It keeps state in memory. Production systems need a durable store and idempotent actions so a retry cannot apply a change twice.

● The thresholds are placeholders. Set your own caps and percentages from your spend and risk tolerance.

● It validates the call but not the data. If upstream numbers are wrong, the agent will act on them.

Runaway spend. Budget APIs act instantly and changes compound. Without a hard ceiling in your own code, a reasoning error becomes a billing event.

Dirty upstream data. Stale CRM records, a broken product feed or a misattributed conversion. The agent can act confidently on wrong inputs and leave a tidy audit trail of well-reasoned bad decisions.

Missing audit trails. If you cannot reconstruct why an agent acted, you cannot fix or defend it. Deloitte's State of AI in the Enterprise reports that close to three-quarters of companies plan to deploy agentic AI within two years, and that only 21% of those companies report having a mature model for agent governance.

● Least-privilege credentials. A separate API key per agent, scoped to exactly what it needs, never a shared admin token.

● Hard caps in code. Daily spend ceilings and a maximum percentage change per adjustment, enforced outside the model.

● Human-in-the-loop thresholds. Define which actions always need approval.

● Log every tool call. Inputs, outputs, errors, model and prompt version, with timestamps you can query.

● Start read-only. Run in suggest mode and compare recommendations with what your team would have done.

A system that pairs a language model with real tools and lets it pursue a goal. It reads state, decides on an action, executes it, observes the result and decides again.

Automation starts from a trigger and follows branches defined in advance. An agent starts from a goal and selects tools at runtime.

Not on current evidence. They handle high-volume, measurable, reversible work well. Strategy, positioning and final brand approval still sit with people.

Function calling, where tools are declared by schema and the model returns matching arguments, and increasingly the Model Context Protocol for connecting tools.

It depends on how quickly errors are detected, whether actions are reversible and what a bad decision costs. Automate the cheap and reversible; supervise the rest.

Wrong tool calls, runaway spend, confident action on dirty data and brand drift at scale. Missing audit trails make all of them harder to diagnose.

An AI marketing agent is less magic than it sounds: a goal, a set of tools and a loop with limits. The model's intelligence matters, but the system around it decides whether it is safe to run. Define the tools narrowly, enforce limits in code, log everything, start read-only and widen autonomy only as the evidence supports it.
