#10105. Distributed LLM Training Across Metros: Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText

Traditionally, training state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) relies on a monolithic cluster of accelerators, where all devices are collocated and tightly coupled via high-bandwidth to constantly synchronize gradients. However, physical and cost constraints make scaling a single slice increasingly difficult.

To scale further, we can connect multiple compute slices across geographically distributed datacenters. However, when training across multiple datacenters over wide-area networks (WAN), standard data-parallel training suffers from severe network stalls because slow WAN transfers cannot be fully overlapped with computation.

To address this, the Google DeepMind and Google Research teams developed DiLoCo (Distributed Low-Communication), a distributed optimization algorithm designed to make training resilient to these low-bandwidth, high-latency connections. In this blog post, we discuss the core concepts of DiLoCo, how it is implemented in MaxText (Google's open-source JAX-based LLM training framework), and the performance benefits it brings to large-scale LLM training on Google Cloud.

DiLoCo was introduced by Google DeepMind in the paper DiLoCo: Distributed Low-Communication Training of Language Models. Instead of synchronizing gradients across all devices at every single training step (as in standard Data Parallelism), DiLoCo partitions training across M independent compute groups, referred to as learners, and allows each learner to perform many local optimization steps independently before communicating across groups.

Despite communicating significantly less frequently (by a factor of H, e.g., 1-2 orders of magnitude less) than standard synchronous training, DiLoCo has been shown to achieve comparable convergence and model quality.

The Vanilla DiLoCo described above reduces total communication volume by a factor of H, however, all learners still transmit their weight deltas for the entire model all at once at every H-th step. On a thin network pipe, that full-model transfer creates a large periodic stall every H steps. This issue is alleviated by Streaming DiLoCo, which is described in the next section.

In multi-slice training, communication within a single slice is handled by high-speed Inter-Chip Interconnects (ICI). When scaling to multiple slices, synchronization across slices must traverse the Data Center Network (DCN) or, if the slices are geographically distributed, wide-area networks (WAN).

Within a single datacenter, DCN bandwidth is high enough that JAX and XLA can often hide standard Data Parallel all-reduce collectives by overlapping gradient communication with backward-pass computation. Across datacenters or congested links, however, bandwidth drops and latency rises sharply. Standard DP stalls at every step waiting for global gradient synchronization, collapsing Model FLOPS Utilization (MFU).

Streaming DiLoCo eliminates this synchronization stall by partitioning the model's parameters into P disjoint fragments. Over the course of H steps, every single fragment in the model is synchronized exactly once, achieving the exact same outer-loop cadence as vanilla DiLoCo without spiky bandwidth utilization.

Using a roofline model (similar to the JAX Scaling Book), we can calculate the network bandwidth required to prevent communication stalls for a model with ∣Θ∣ parameters (at b bytes per parameter, e.g., b=2 for bfloat16) and per-step compute time tstep=tfwd+tbwd. For example, the standard Data Parallel algorithm transfers 2∣Θ∣b bytes per sync via an all-reduce collective, which is a distributed reduce-scatter where each node reduces a different part, followed by an all-gather so all participants receive all parts. To achieve zero communication stalls, we overlap the reduce-scatter of the gradients with the backward pass, which produces them and takes tbwd time. Therefore, the peak required bandwidth is Bdp ≈ 2∣Θ∣b / tbwd.

Worked Example (Qwen3-32B in bfloat16, tstep​≈6.2s, P=H=65):

To evaluate distributed efficiency under realistic network bottlenecks, we benchmarked Qwen3-32B (64 decoder layers, partitioned into P = 65 fragments with H = 65) across 2× TPU v5p-32 slices (dcn_diloco_parallelism=2) using built-in Linux traffic-control (tc) bandwidth shaping to limit inter-slice bandwidth across three network regimes: Unthrottled (Line Rate), 30 Gbit/s aggregate (Moderate Congestion), and 10 Gbit/s aggregate (Severe Congestion).

A common question with low-communication training is whether allowing learners to train independently between synchronizations degrades model quality—especially on Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, where independent updates could cause expert routers to drift apart.

We evaluated Streaming DiLoCo against synchronous Data Parallel pre-training across two MoE architectures in MaxText (Qwen3-30B-A3B and Gemma4-26B-A4B) over 4,000 training steps:

Empirical results confirm that Streaming DiLoCo preserves—and even slightly improves—validation loss:

MaxText provides a native JAX and Flax NNX implementation of both Vanilla and Streaming DiLoCo—requiring no external central parameter servers.

You can enable Streaming DiLoCo in your MaxText YAML configuration or CLI overrides by setting enable_diloco: true and enable_streaming_diloco: true. As a best practice, set both num_diloco_fragments (P) and diloco_sync_period (H) to N_layers + 1 so that exactly one fragment is synchronized per training step (Δh = 1):

In MaxText, diloco.py splits each global batch across the M learners so each learner processes GBS / M tokens per step. To overlap cross-slice communication with TPU computation in Streaming DiLoCo, weight deltas for the scheduled fragment are offloaded to M-way sharded syncer workers running on the learner host CPUs. Rather than routing through a single central coordinator, the host-CPU syncer shards execute a distributed all-reduce across the network in the background to average the fragment deltas while the TPUs continue running inner-loop training steps. Each syncer shard then applies the outer Nesterov momentum update locally and writes the updated fragment weights back to its colocated TPU learner. To dive deeper or launch your first run:

Streaming DiLoCo in MaxText decouples large-scale LLM pre-training from the requirement of a single, monolithic, high-bandwidth fabric. By combining use of an inner and outer optimizer with fine-grained parameter streaming, engineering teams can pool TPU slices across congested datacenter networks or geographically distributed metros—sustaining high hardware utilization without compromising model convergence. Streaming DiLoCo is available today in MaxText v0.2.5 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Try it today for experimenting with distributed training workloads.

#10101. How AI-Powered Cameras Are Changing Content Creation: What Developers Should Know

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating high-quality visual content once meant specialized cameras, technical knowledge and hours of editing. Today, software does a growing share of that work. Cameras recognize subjects, adjust exposure, stabilize footage and hand clips to editing tools that transcribe, reframe and tag them.

Digital photography made images easy to capture, review and share. Smartphones then folded capture, editing and publishing into one device. The next shift was computational photography: instead of relying only on the lens and sensor, the device combines several captures and processes them in software.

High dynamic range (HDR) imaging is the clearest example. A camera captures frames at different exposures and merges them so both bright skies and dark foregrounds keep detail. Research indexed on PubMed, published in IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence, examines how deep learning has advanced HDR imaging, particularly in reconstructing visual information across different exposure levels.

Machine learning extends this approach from merging pixels to understanding them, which is what lets a camera decide what is the subject and what is the background.

Most AI camera features fall into a few groups.

Because of these failure modes, a pipeline should check what the camera produced before running expensive downstream steps.

A cheap pre-check can reject a frame that is too soft or badly exposed before you spend time on transcription, upscaling or model inference. The script below uses numpy and Pillow. It measures sharpness as the variance of a Laplacian, a standard blur indicator, and measures exposure as the share of crushed and clipped pixels.

The test builds a detailed random frame and a blurred copy, then checks that the sharp frame scores far higher, that a mostly black frame is flagged as underexposed and that a mostly white frame is flagged as overexposed:

Run the tests with python3 test_frame_quality.py. In our run, the detailed test frame scored about 33,000 for sharpness and its blurred copy scored under 2.

Traditional capture means holding a camera, propping up a phone or setting up equipment. Wearable cameras let creators record from their own point of view while their hands stay free, which suits travel video, instructional content, outdoor activities and behind-the-scenes footage.

Camera-equipped smart glasses are one form of this. Several makers sell them, and consumer listings such as ai glasses with camera show how the category is presented to buyers. Depending on the device, users start recording with physical controls or voice commands. Specifications differ widely, so compare field of view, storage, battery life and software support before choosing one.

For developers, wearables change the input. Footage is shaky, framed by head movement and often recorded outdoors, which is exactly where a quality check and stabilization matter. Battery capacity, camera position, storage and recording duration also limit extended productions. Wearables are an additional option for perspectives that are hard to reach otherwise, not a replacement for traditional cameras.

Editing involves reviewing clips, correcting color, adjusting audio and preparing videos for each platform. AI tools automate several repetitive parts of that work:

Creative judgment stays human. Choosing meaningful moments, shaping narrative flow and keeping a consistent visual style are decisions software cannot reliably make. Real-time assistance during recording will depend on hardware, software and environmental conditions.

As cameras get smaller, responsible recording matters more. Wearable devices can capture footage without the obvious movements of a handheld camera, so people nearby may not know recording is happening. The European Data Protection Board has highlighted transparency, personal data protection and recording awareness as important considerations for camera-equipped smart glasses.

Authenticity is the second challenge. AI tools can enhance an image, but heavy alteration can also mislead. The National Institute of Standards and Technology has examined technical approaches to digital content transparency, including provenance tracking, watermarking and synthetic-content detection. On the provenance side, the C2PA coalition publishes an open standard called Content Credentials, which helps show where content came from and what edits it has undergone.

Cameras will likely get better at faster subject recognition, more efficient automated editing and stronger low-light performance as computer vision and on-device computing advance. Wearables may widen first-person storytelling, and editing tools may make multi-platform publishing simpler. Technical gains alone will not decide quality, though. Originality, storytelling and thoughtful creative decisions still do.

Not always. Toolkits such as ML Kit face detection and Apple's Vision framework run image analysis on the device, which reduces delay and keeps frames local.

It narrows the gap in many conditions, but optics, sensor size and control still matter for demanding work, and AI enhancement can introduce artifacts of its own.

Add an early quality gate that checks sharpness and exposure, as in the script above, and calibrate its thresholds on your own footage.

It depends on location and context. Regulators such as the European Data Protection Board have raised transparency and consent concerns, so check local rules and be open about recording.

Provenance standards such as C2PA Content Credentials aim to show a file's origin and edit history, though they only help when tools and platforms support them.

This article is for general informational purposes only and isn't legal advice.

#10091. How to Build an Accessible Multi Step Form: A Practical Guide With a Vanilla JavaScript Example

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A long form can feel like a chore before anyone types a word. Splitting it into smaller steps lets people focus on a few related questions at a time.

The code looks simple: show one section, hide the rest and add a button. The difficult parts are validation, keyboard use, focus and what happens when JavaScript doesn't run. This guide builds an accessible multi step form with HTML and plain JavaScript. It also reproduces a bug that can leave visitors stuck on the first step.

Multiple steps make sense when questions fall into clear groups, such as project details, timing and contact information. A form with only an email address and a message usually doesn't need them.

Splitting a form doesn't make unnecessary questions useful. Remove those first. More steps also aren't a guarantee of more completed forms, so test the change with your own audience.

The W3C tutorial on multiple page forms advises dividing long forms into logical stages and telling users about their progress. Our example applies those ideas within a single page.

Without JavaScript, all three groups remain visible and Send works as an ordinary submit button. The Back button stays hidden because it has no job in that view. This approach is called progressive enhancement: start with a working form, then add the step behavior.

The action value is a placeholder. Point it to a real server endpoint in your project. If your stylesheet sets a display value on fieldsets, check that it doesn't override the hidden attribute.

Save the following code as form.js in the same folder:

The form has one submit button. Its label reads Continue until the last step. The submit handler decides whether to show an error, open the next step or let the browser send the data. Pressing Enter in a text field follows the same path.

Back is a regular button, so it never submits anything. The answers remain in their fields because the code only hides each step. It doesn't remove or rebuild it.

To test its purpose, I removed only this line, filled in the first field and pressed Enter in headless Chromium 158. The form stayed on step 1. The console reported messages like these:

Before firing the submit event, the browser checked the entire form. The empty required fields on later steps were invalid, but they were hidden. Submission was blocked, so our handler never had a chance to open step 2.

Setting noValidate stops that automatic check during submission. It doesn't disable checkValidity() or reportValidity(), so our code can validate only the visible step. Because the line lives in JavaScript, the browser's normal validation still applies if the script never loads.

For more detail on native validation rules and methods, see SitePoint's Complete Guide to HTML Forms and Constraint Validation.

The test intercepted the request, so it checked what the browser sent rather than a real server. It also doesn't show how other browsers behave.

Each legend includes the current step number, and the page title changes with it. After someone moves to another step, focus goes to the new legend. This gives keyboard users a clear starting point. The code doesn't move focus during the first page load.

The negative tabindex lets JavaScript focus a legend without adding it to the normal Tab order. Keep the focus outline visible, or replace it with an equally clear style. Screen reader behavior varies, so test with the browser and screen reader combinations your visitors use.

Native validation messages are useful for a small example. A production form may need persistent error text beside each field. If so, connect each message to its field with aria-describedby, set aria-invalid when needed and clear both after the error is fixed.

Also avoid asking for the same information twice. WCAG 2.2 Redundant Entry requires previously entered information needed again in the same process to be filled in or available to select. There are exceptions, including certain security needs and information that is no longer valid. This example alone doesn't make a whole website WCAG compliant.

Browser checks can be changed or bypassed. Your server should validate required fields, allowed choices, lengths and formats again. It should also handle spam, protect against cross site request forgery where relevant and escape stored values when displaying them.

Before release, try the complete form with only a keyboard. Check mobile layouts, zoom, browser autofill, Back and Forward navigation, slow connections and server errors. Make sure repeated clicks don't create duplicate records.

SitePoint's practical workflow for testing JavaScript forms is a helpful next step when you're preparing a broader test plan.

The example also leaves out conditional questions. If one answer can remove a later step, calculate the active steps before updating the progress label. Decide how to handle old answers from inactive branches, and enforce the same rules on the server.

Custom code gives you control over the markup and behavior, but your team must maintain validation, delivery, security and testing. A form builder can reduce the amount of interface code you manage. For example, LeadGen App lists multi step forms, conditional logic and website embedding among its features.

Whichever route you choose, test the finished form instead of relying on a feature list.

An accessible multi step form needs more than a few hidden sections. Start with semantic HTML, validate each visible step and keep progress and focus clear.

Pay special attention to required fields that appear later. A single hidden field can stop the first button from working, even when the visible answer is correct. Test that case, then check the whole journey with real users and a real server before launch.

#10081. Node.js Hosting Between Serverless and VPS: Predictable Costs Without Cold Starts

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The first sign of trouble is rarely a crash. It is the invoice.

A small team ships a Node.js app on a serverless platform because it is quick to set up. Traffic grows, and the bill grows with it, in ways that are hard to forecast. Some visitors also wait a beat longer for their first response, because the function has to start up before it can answer.

The usual alternative is a virtual private server (VPS). The price is fixed, but the work is not. Someone has to patch the operating system (OS), configure the web server, renew SSL certificates and keep backups running.

Between the two sits a third option: managed hosting for Node.js. Here is how the three compare, and how to choose.

Platforms such as AWS Lambda charge for what runs. Lambda's event functions are priced on the number of requests and on duration, measured in GB-seconds, and the memory you choose sets the CPU and the cost per second.

That suits small or spiky workloads. A new app with little traffic can sit inside the free tier, which covers one million requests and 400,000 GB-seconds a month.

The surprises come with growth. Billed duration is multiplied by the number of requests, so even small inefficiencies in a handler can turn into significant costs at scale.

Then there is the cold start. When the platform has to create a new execution environment, the first request waits. AWS's own decision guide puts Lambda cold starts at roughly 100 milliseconds to over 1 second.

Since August 2025, AWS bills for that initialization (INIT) phase across all function configurations, so a cold start now shows up on the invoice as well as in the response time.

AWS's own analysis found they occur in under 1% of invocations, so steady traffic makes them far less. For a checkout page that does hit one, it is still a pause the customer feels.

There are fixes. Provisioned concurrency keeps functions initialized and ready, but it is billed for the time it is enabled. Newer options let one execution environment handle several requests at once, which means fewer cold starts.

Limits matter, too. A Lambda function can run for 15 minutes at most, and accounts start with a default of 1,000 concurrent executions. Long-running jobs need a different home.

A VPS fixes the pricing problem. A plan costs the same every month, whatever traffic does.

On an unmanaged VPS, the team owns everything above the hardware. That means operating system updates, a firewall, a web server such as NGINX in front of the app, a process manager such as PM2 to restart it if it crashes, SSL certificate renewals and backups that someone has to test.

None of it is exotic. All of it is a job. Left undone, it is how small teams end up with unpatched servers and no way to restore one.

For a team without a sysadmin, the real price of a VPS is the hours.

Managed Node.js hosting sits between the two. The app runs on a persistent infrastructure, so there is no scale-to-zero and no cold start between requests. The price is a flat monthly plan. The provider handles the work around it.

Cloudways Velocity is one example. It patches the OS, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically.

The stack is NGINX and PM2, pre-configured with caching, and PostgreSQL is provisioned on all plans. Deployment is by Git push with built-in CI/CD, and a WAF, malware protection, DDoS mitigation and Cloudflare Enterprise CDN are included.

It ships presets for 12 project types, including Next.js, Remix, Express, Fastify, Astro, Nuxt, Angular and n8n. Anything that builds to SSR or a static bundle and starts with a Node command can run under "Other". Runtime versions are selectable, with Node.js LTS 22.x and 24.x listed.

The trade-off is scaling. Velocity scales vertically: you pick a server size and move up when the app outgrows it. A traffic spike beyond that server's capacity is a capacity problem, not something the platform absorbs by adding instances.

Look at the traffic first. If it comes in bursts with long quiet gaps, serverless often wins. If it is steady, a server is usually simpler to reason about.

Take one handler and keep the assumptions the same throughout. Each request runs for half a second on 1 GB of memory. The rates are AWS's published x86 prices: $0.20 per million requests and $0.0000166667 per GB-second, after the free tier, as shown earlier.

Now compare Velocity's plans, which use a pay-as-you-go model billed on an hourly basis according to the selected plan, ranging from Starter at $20 a month (2 GB RAM, 2 vCPU, 5 apps) to Business at $400 (64 GB RAM, 16 vCPU, 150 apps). More importantly billing is strictly tied to the server plan rather than incoming traffic, a sudden spike in visits will not automatically raise your hourly compute rate.

The Lambda figures leave out API gateway charges, data transfer and logging. A single server has a ceiling, so load-test before assuming one plan carries 100 million requests.

And Velocity has metered extras: $0.033/GB for offsite backup storage, $0.02/GB for CDN bandwidth beyond the plan's allocation, and $1 per 10 GB of extra disk.

If you want to test managed hosting, you can try Cloudways Velocity during Black Friday with the promo code BFCM802 for 80% off for 2 months plus unlimited migrations, valid through December 4, 2026.

The point is the shape, not a winner. Serverless cost tracks usage. A server's cost tracks its size. Choose the one you can forecast.

The strongest case is also the most common one: steady traffic, a small team and a budget that has to be forecast.

When cloud hosting costs have to fit a budget, a flat monthly plan beats a meter. Velocity's plans give you Node.js hosting with predictable pricing, so finance can see the number in advance and it does not move with every spike.

Because the server stays on, there is no cold start between requests, and the first visitor after a quiet spell is not left waiting.

Cloudways says Velocity patches the operating system, manages SSL and domain mapping, and runs backups automatically. The people who built the product keep building it.

APIs, full-stack apps and automation workflows such as n8n need a process that stays up. A persistent server is built for exactly that.

When the app outgrows its server, you move up a size, and you can move back down if you don't need it.

For most small teams, that adds up to a simple choice. Velocity trades the theoretical ceiling of serverless for something many teams value more: costs they can forecast and an app that stays ready, without a DevOps hire.

Teams whose traffic is extreme, or who need code running at the edge, may still prefer a specialist platform, but that is the exception.

Serverless is strongest when work is irregular. A VPS is strongest when you want control and have the time. Managed Node.js hosting suits the large group in between: teams with steady traffic, a small headcount and no appetite for server upkeep.

Teams looking for managed hosting can explore Cloudways Velocity to deploy and scale Node.js applications efficiently.

#10069. European Commission confirms personal data stolen in AI-assisted cyberattack

EU Academy names and email addresses exposed, with users warned via website notice rather than contacted directly

The European Commission has confirmed that hackers made off with personal data in the AI-assisted cyberattack disclosed last week, but has relied on a website notice rather than contacting affected users directly.

Speaking at a press briefing on Monday, Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier confirmed that EU Academy, an online learning platform offering courses on EU policies and initiatives, was among the systems compromised during the cyber campaign targeting the bloc's executive arm.

The stolen data includes the names and email addresses of people outside the EU institutions, though the Commission has yet to figure out how many users are affected or whether other personal information was compromised. Public access to the platform has been disabled as a precaution.

Despite confirming the theft, the Commission hasn't contacted affected individuals directly. Instead, it has posted a notice on the EU Academy website, a decision Regnier defended when quizzed by reporters on Monday.

"There was no reach out to individual users, but rather a general message to all users on the website out of transparency," Regnier said.

The notice warns users that they could find themselves on the receiving end of spearphishing attacks, impersonation attempts, and spam as a result of the breach. The Commission is also advising users not to hand over personal information to anyone who contacts them claiming to be involved in EU Academy activities.

The trouble with notifying users via the EU Academy website is that they've already been told not to come back until January. A separate notice says the platform will be offline from November 11 through January 14, 2027, while the Commission rebuilds it. When it reopens, users will have to register again, and any record of their previous coursework won't be transferred.

The Register has asked the Commission whether the downtime and the requirement to create new accounts are connected to the cyberattack.A spokesperson didn't answer those points.

The Commission has informed the European Data Protection Supervisor (EDPS), the watchdog responsible for policing EU institutions' handling of personal data.

The EDPS told The Register that the Commission had notified it of the incident in stages, but declined to say whether it considered the Commission's approach to notifying affected users sufficient.

"I can confirm that the EDPS has been informed and notified in phases by the European Commission," a spokesperson said. "Our colleagues remain in regular contact with EC services on the cyberattack and its implication. This is an ongoing case, so I cannot tell you more at this stage on the actions the EDPS might take as supervisory authority."

The Commission first publicly acknowledged the cyberattack last Thursday, almost a month after suspicious activity was detected on October 22. Several internal applications were temporarily taken offline, and while most had since been restored, some remained unavailable at the time of Thursday's briefing.

The Commission has confirmed the attackers used an AI-assisted hacking tool, and the incident comes amid mounting concerns over ARTEX, an AI-powered penetration testing framework that security researchers have linked to large-scale cyber espionage campaigns.

More than a month after the Commission first spotted the intrusion, Regnier was unwilling to say on Monday whether its security teams had finally seen off the hackers.

"As long as the investigation is ongoing, I will not make any conclusive statements at this stage on where we are," he said. ®

#10067. Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

In our previous posts - Easy FunctionGemma finetuning with Tunix on Google TPUs and Autonomous LLM post-training with Tunix on TPUs - we showed how Google Tunix streamlines Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and single-turn Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on Cloud TPUs. Real-world AI agents, however, rarely finish their job in a single turn. They have to reason over partial observations, invoke tools, observe environment feedback, and adapt their strategy across multiple steps. So in this post, we will walk through how to train a multi-turn, tool-using LLM agent using Tunix's agentic reinforcement learning pipeline on Cloud TPU v6e-8. You can find the complete code in this GitHub repository.

We will revisit the Plane Strike board game from an earlier TensorFlow/JAX reinforcement learning blog, replacing the custom MLP policy with Gemma 4 E2B in thinking mode on a single Cloud TPU v6e-8 host. Plane Strike is played on a 6x6 grid (A1 to F6) where one 8-cell plane is hidden in one of 4 orientations. Each turn, the agent sees the result of its previous strike and the raw visible board (. untried, X hit, O miss), thinks through candidate placements, and calls the strike(cell="...") tool to strike one cell. The game ends when all 8 plane cells are hit, or after 23 strikes.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Although google/gemma-4-E2B-it has decent spatial reasoning and tool calling capabilities, out of the box, it wins only 9.4% of games on held-out boards (averaging 22.44 strikes). After 100 steps of agentic GRPO with Tunix, its win rate reaches 95% while cutting the average game length to ~16 strikes.

We set up the multi-turn RL pipeline in four modular components:

Let's look at the key steps for each piece.

In env.py, we subclass Tunix's BaseTaskEnv to implement PlaneStrikeEnv. Each environment instance receives a task dictionary with a seed that fixes the hidden plane position so all 8 rollouts in a GRPO group play the exact same board:

On each turn, _step_impl evaluates the struck cell index against the hidden board, updates the visible grid, computes the step reward (summed into the trajectory-level reward over the episode), and returns EnvStepResult(observation, reward, done, info).

For standard multi-tool agents, tunix.rl.agentic also provides ready-to-use ToolEnvironment, ToolAgent and ToolManager abstractions out of the box so we don't have to write custom message/observation plumbing unless we need custom turn control.

In Gemma 4, tool schemas are declared in the system prompt inside <|tool>declaration:..., and a model turn that calls a tool ends with:

<|tool_response> is one of Gemma 4's native stop tokens: the model pauses there and waits for response:strike{value:<|"|>...<|"|>}, then continues generating its next thought and tool call within the same assistant turn.

In strike_tool.py, we define StrikeTool(BaseTool) and subclass Tunix's Gemma4ChatTemplateParser to append the tool declaration to the system prompt and render "tool" messages as in-turn responses:

In agent.py, PlaneStrikeAgent(ConversationAgentBase) translates between environment observations and model messages.

When the model finishes a turn, tool_strike ignores everything inside <|channel>thought... and parses the final answer after ; only a final answer with a single valid strike call counts as a valid board coordinate (otherwise INVALID_ACTION is returned). If the model stopped at <|tool_response>, the next board observation is appended with role="tool" so the model continues its turn:

In train_planestrike.py, we configure Tunix's asynchronous vllm rollout engine, RLEngine, and GRPOLearner:

Multi-turn agentic RL is often bottlenecked by variable-length rollout generation, so we set rollout_vllm_server_mode to true to enable continuous asynchronous multi-turn rollouts via vllm-tpu and enable_prefix_caching to true to true to cache the growing multi-turn conversation history. Since we are using a v6e-8 single host, the actor model, the reference model, and the rollout workers share the 8 chips.

We then pass our actor, tokenizer, ClusterConfig, GRPOConfig (loss_algo="gspo-token", advantage_estimator="rloo", exact_token_continuity=True to preserve exact token IDs across multi-turn rollouts without retokenization drift), agent, and environment into RLEngine and PlaneStrikeGRPOLearner:

All of these enable us to launch the 100-step training job with:

Over 100 steps (8 boards x 8 games per step), the agent learns both reliable tool calling and spatial deduction:

With Tunix's tunix.rl.agentic abstractions, vllm-tpu, and GRPOLearner, multi-turn, tool-using agentic RL on Cloud TPUs is easy and efficient. Check out the planestrike-gemma4-rl GitHub repository and read the Tunix documentation to train your own agentic model with Tunix and Google TPUs.

#10063. CrowdStrike splashes $44B on Zscaler, customers brace for the bill

Record-breaking cybersecurity deal promises tighter integration, but sysadmins fear higher prices and vendor lock-in

CrowdStrike has agreed to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in what would be the biggest cybersecurity acquisition on record, and customers are already bracing for pricier renewals.

The all-stock deal, announced on Monday, will see Zscaler shareholders receive 1.135 CrowdStrike shares for every share they own, representing a 34 percent premium on Friday's closing price. The transaction dwarfs even Google's $32 billion acquisition of cloud security upstart Wiz.

Buying Zscaler gives CrowdStrike a sizeable foothold in the network security market, where it will be going up against the likes of Palo Alto Networks and Fortinet. The plan is to combine Falcon's endpoint protection capabilities with Zscaler's zero-trust network security technology, giving customers a single platform for securing devices and the connections between them.

Naturally, there's an AI angle to the biggest cyber deal in history. CrowdStrike reckons owning both technologies will allow it to keep tabs on what AI agents are doing on corporate devices, where they're connecting, and what data they're getting their hands on.

CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz described the proposed combination as "cybersecurity’s operating system."

The acquisition also gives CrowdStrike's salespeople plenty to get excited about. Around 8,000 Zscaler customers don't currently buy CrowdStrike's wares, and the companies already have more than 2,000 customers in common. CrowdStrike reckons it can boost its annualized revenue by around $1 billion within 30 months of closing the deal by selling more products to the two companies' existing customers.

Some of those customers, however, don't sound particularly keen on being cross-sold to. Over on Reddit's r/sysadmin subreddit, news of the purchase was met with a familiar set of concerns from IT professionals who've lived through big-ticket enterprise software acquisitions before.

"As a zscaler customer, this does worry me. Cost increase, forced move to Falcon, integration issues, etc," wrote one sysadmin.

Another, whose organization already uses both vendors' products, added: "I think they're both great products but I imagine they'll jack up the price for our next renewal. Fun."

CrowdStrike intends to make Zscaler's products available through its Falcon Flex licensing scheme, which allows customers to commit to a certain level of spending and pick and choose the products they deploy. Monday's acquisition announcement didn't spell out what the deal would mean for customers who want to continue using Zscaler alongside a rival endpoint security product.

Others raised concerns about the fate of Red Canary, the managed detection and response specialist snapped up by Zscaler in August 2025. CrowdStrike already operates a competing service in Falcon Complete.

There may also be some nervous employees at both companies. CrowdStrike is targeting $500 million in annual cost savings within two years of completing the acquisition, identifying datacenter infrastructure, supplier spending, and wider operations as areas where it can trim the fat. Neither company disclosed whether that would involve job cuts.

Then there's the matter of putting more of the world's corporate security infrastructure in the hands of CrowdStrike, whose botched July 2024 software update took down an estimated 8.5 million Windows machines, grounding flights and disrupting hospitals, banks, and businesses worldwide. The incident gave IT departments a rather painful lesson in the risks of relying heavily on a single technology supplier.

The deal is expected to close in the second half of CrowdStrike's fiscal 2028, assuming regulators and Zscaler shareholders give it the green light. Zscaler founder and CEO Jay Chaudhry will remain in charge of the business and take a seat on CrowdStrike's board.

CrowdStrike reckons the combined business will have more than $10 billion in annual recurring revenue when the deal closes. Judging by the reaction from some customers, they'll be keeping a particularly close eye on how much of that revenue comes from them. ®
